Mixed ℓ \ell -adic complexes for schemes over number fields
Sophie Morel

Abstract
If X X is a variety over a number field, Annette Huber has defined in [ Hub97 ] a category of “horizontal” (or “almost everywhere unramified”) ℓ \ell -adic complexes and ℓ \ell -adic perverse sheaves on X X . For such objects, the notion of weights makes sense (in the sense of Deligne, see [ Del80 ] ), just as in the case of varieties over finite fields. However, contrary to what happens in that last case, mixed perverse sheaves (or mixed locally constant sheaves) on X X do not have a weight filtration in general, even when X X is a point. The goal of this paper is to show how to avoid this problem by working directly in the derived category of the abelian category of perverse sheaves that do admit a weight filtration. As an application, the methods of [ Mor08 ] to calculate the intermediate extension of a pure perverse sheaf apply over any finitely generated field, and not just over a finite field.



1
1

1

2010
Mathematics Subject Classification.
Primary 14F43, 14G25; Secondary 13D09

1
Introduction

Let
k
be a field of finite type over its prime subfield,
let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
,
and let
\ell
be a prime number invertible in
k
. In her article

[
Hub97
]
Annette Huber introduced a category
\Dbm(X)=D^{b}_{m}(X,E)

of mixed horizontal
\ell
-adic sheaves on
X
, where
E
is an
algebraic extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
.
The idea of
[
Hub97
]

is to consider the category of
\ell
-adic complexes on
X
that
extend to a constructible
\ell
-adic complex on a model
\mathcal{X}

of
X
over a normal scheme
\mathcal{U}
of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
and with field
of fractions
k
;
we also want the
morphisms between complexes to extend to
\mathcal{X}
. There is a
natural definition of weights (in the sense of Deligne’s
[
Del80
]
) on such
complexes, by considering their restriction to the fibers of
\mathcal{X}
over
closed points of
\mathcal{U}
. So we have a notion of pure sheaves, and mixed
complexes are defined (as in
[
Del80
]
) as those complexes whose cohomology
sheaves have a filtration with pure quotients.

By sections 2 and 3 of
[
Hub97
]
, the 6 operations (usual and exceptional
direct and inverse images, tensor products and internal Homs) exist on these
categories of complexes. Moreover, it is shown in 2.5 and 3.2 of
[
Hub97
]

that the category
D^{b}_{m}(X)
has a (self-dual) perverse t-structure,
whose heart
\Perv_{m}(X)
is called the category of horizontal mixed perverse
sheaves on
X
.

Also, the results of chapters 4 and 5 of
Beilinson-Bernstein-Deligne’s book
[
BBD82
]
about
the t-exactness (or perverse cohomological amplitude) of the 6 functors,
and the way these 6 functors affect weights, can be extended to our
situation thanks to
Deligne’s generic base change theorem (SGA 4 1/2 [Th. finitude]
section 2), see for example 3.4 and 3.5 of
[
Hub97
]
.

Finally, there is a notion of weight filtration on an object of
\Perv_{m}(X)

(see
[
Hub97
]
3.7); it is an increasing filtration whose quotients are
pure perverse sheaves
of increasing weights. This filtration is unique if it exists (
[
Hub97
]

3.8), but unfortunately
it doesn’t always exist, unless
k
is a finite field. As noted
in the remark below
[
Hub97
]
3.8, the category of horizontal mixed
perverse sheaves on
X
admitting a weight filtration is a full abelian
subcategory
\Perv_{mf}(X)
of
\Perv_{m}(X)
which is stable by subquotients,
but it is not stable by extensions.

As a consequence, if we start from a horizontal mixed perverse sheaf that
does have a weight filtration and apply some sheaf operations, then it
is not clear that the perverse cohomology sheaves of the resulting
mixed complex will still have weight filtrations. (Although we
would certainly expect that to be the case.)
For example,
this is a problem if we want to generalize the arguments of
[
Mor08
]
, that
gives among other things a formula for
the intersection complex of
X
.

The goal of this paper is to give a solution to this problem, inspired
by Beilinson’s theorem that, if
k
is a finite field, then the derived
category of
\Perv_{m}(X)
is canonically equivalent to
D^{b}_{m}(X)

(see
[
Beĭ87b
]
,
[
Beĭ87a
]
; note that Beilinson’s result is more general).
Beilinson also gives a way to reconstruct the derived direct image
functors from their perverse versions, and formulas adapted to perverse
sheaves for the unipotent
nearby
and vanishing cycles functors. Building on this, Morihiko Saito has
shown in
[
Sai90
]
and
[
Sai91
]
how to recover the other operations
(inverse images, tensor products and internal
\Hom
s) using only
perverse sheaves.

In this paper, we will follow the ideas of Beilinson and M. Saito to construct
all the sheaf operations on the bounded derived categories of the
categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
. The main point, which is taken as an axiom
in
[
Sai91
]
, is the fact that these categories are stable by
perverse direct images; in section
6.3
, we show how to deduce it
from Deligne’s weight-monodromy theorem. Another difficulty is to state
all the compatibilities that the sheaf operations should satisfy. We have
chosen to use the formalism of crossed functors (“foncteurs croisés”),
originally due to Deligne and developed by Voevodsky and Ayoub. In
order to check that the constructions of Beilinson and M. Saito do fit
into this formalism, we have had to rewrite some of them. (Another
reason is that the categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
satisfy assumptions that are
slightly different from the axioms of
[
Sai91
]
, and so certain proofs
become simpler, and at least one proof has to be totally changed. However,
most of the constructions are very similar to the ones in
[
Sai91
]
.)

Here is a quick description of the different parts of the paper.
Section 1 is the introduction, and section 2 contains reminders about

\ell
-adic perverse sheaves, the realization functor and a quick
summary of the beginning of
Huber’s article
[
Hub97
]
, in particular the definition of the main
object of study
\Perv_{mf}(X)
. In section 3, we state the main results
of the paper, first informally and then using the language of crossed
functors. Section 4 gives a list of functors that obviously preserve the
categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.
Section 5 contains reminders about Beilinson’s construction of unipotent
nearby and vanishing cycles.
In section 6, we state the form of Deligne’s
weight-monodromy theorem that we will use, and deduce the crucial fact that
perverse direct images also preserve the categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
; we also
give an application to complexes with
support in a closed subscheme, that was already noted in 2.2.1 of

[
Beĭ87b
]
and Theorem 5.6 of
[
Sai91
]
.
Section 7 gives the proof
of the first main theorem (Theorem
3.2.4
, concerning the
existence of the four operations
f^{*},f_{*},f_{!},f^{!}
), and section 8 gives
the proof of the second main theorem (Theorem
3.2.12
,
about the existence of tensor products and internal
\Hom
s).
Section 9 shows how the results of this article imply that we can extend
the formalism of weight truncation functors defined in
[
Mor08
]
.
Finally, the appendix gives a review of filtered derived categories and
f-categories (i.e. filtered triangulated categories), with a focus on
the compatibility between the realization functor and derived functors.

Here are some conventions that will be used throughout the paper :

-

As we are considering sheaves for the étale topology or
proétale topology, we
only care about schemes up to universal homeomorphism. So we will allow
ourselves to specify a closed subscheme of a scheme
X
by giving only the
underlying closed subset.

-

We are mostly interested in the triangulated versions of the
sheaf operations, so we will denote them without the usual “R”’s or
“L”’s. For example, the derived direct image functors will simply be denoted
by
f_{*}
, and we will similarly write
f^{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
for the
other direct and image inverse functors, seen as functors between the
triangulated categories of complexes of sheaves. The only exception we will
make is for the functor
R\Hom
(in an abelian category), in order to distinguish it
from
\Hom
.

-

All the schemes will be assumed to be excellent and separated,
and all the morphisms will be assumed to be of finite type. (We are only
interested in schemes that are of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
or over a field,
and these schemes are automatically excellent.) If we write “scheme
over
k
”, where
k
is a field, we will mean “separated field of
finite type over
k
”.
Also,
the letter
\ell
always stands for a prime number invertible over
all the schemes considered.

-

If
\mathcal{C}
is an additive category, we will denote by
\Co(\mathcal{C})
(resp.
\Cpl(\mathcal{C})
,
\Cmn(\mathcal{C})
,
\Cb(\mathcal{C})
) the additive
category of (cohomological) complexes of objects of
\mathcal{C}
(resp. of bounded below complexes, bounded above complexes, bounded
complexes); we also denote by
\Ko(\mathcal{C})
(resp.
\Kpl(\mathcal{C})
,
\Kmn(\mathcal{C})
,
\Kb(\mathcal{C})
) the corresponding triangulated categories
of complexes modulo homotopy.

-

If
\mathcal{A}
is a quasi-abelian category (for example of abelian category), we denote by
\D(\mathcal{A})
(resp.
\Dpl(\mathcal{A})
,
\D^{-}(\mathcal{A})
,

\Db(\mathcal{A})
) the derived category (resp. bounded below derived category, bounded above derived category, bounded derived) of
\mathcal{A}
,
defined as the quotient of
\Ko(\mathcal{A})
(resp.
\Kpl(\mathcal{A})
,
\Kmn(\mathcal{A})
,
\Kb(\mathcal{A})
) by the triangulated subcategory of exact complexes.

2
Horizontal perverse sheaves

In this section, we recall the definition of
\ell
-adic constructible
complexes and
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves,
the construction of the realization
functor from the bounded derived category of perverse sheaves to
the category of constructible complexes, and finally
the definition by A. Huber of horizontal

\ell
-adic complexes and horizontal perverse sheaves, as well as the
mixed versions.

2.1
\ell
-adic complexes

Let
X
be a scheme and
E
be an algebraic extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
.
If we want to stay in the familiar framework of triangulated categories
(and avoid
\infty
-categories), there are two approaches to the
category of bounded constructible étale
E
-complexes on
X
that
work at the level of generality that we need : Ekedahl’s approach in
[
Eke90
]

(see also Fargues’s paper
[
Far09
]
for some complements)
and the Bhatt-Scholze definition via the proétale site in
[
BS15
]
.
The second works in a more general setting, and it is known to be
equivalent to the first when they both apply.
While we could make the constructions that we need work with both
approaches, we
will mostly stick to the Bhatt-Scholze approach, because it makes the
homological algebra simpler.

Remark 2.1.1
.

In his article
[
Eke90
]
, Ekedahl makes the assumption that
the scheme
X
is of finite type over a regular scheme of dimension
\leq 1
.
The reason for this is that the necessary theorems for torsion étale
sheaves were only available in this setting at the time. Since then, Gabber
has proved the finiteness theorem (see Exposé XIII of
[
ILO14
]
), the
absolute purity theorem (see
[
Fuj02
]
or III.3 of
[
ILO14
]
)
and the existence of a
dualizing complex (see Exposé XVII of
[
ILO14
]
) in the more general
setting considered here, so the results of
[
Eke90
]
extend to this setting.

Let us review quickly the construction of
Bhatt and Scholze via the proétale
site
X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}
of
X
(see
[
BS15
]
) :
The category
\Dbc(X,E)
is defined as
a the full subcategory of the category
\D(X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)
of sheaves of

E
-modules on
X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}
(definition 4.1.1 of
[
BS15
]
)
whose objects are bounded complexes with constructible cohomology sheaves,
where a proétale sheaf
\mathcal{F}
of
E
-vector spaces is called
constructible

if
X
has a finite stratification
(Z_{i})_{i\in I}
by locally closed subschemes
such that each

\mathcal{F}_{|Z_{i}}
is lisse, i.e. locally (in the proétale topology)
free of finite rank;
see definitions 6.8.6 and 6.8.8 of
[
BS15
]
. By propositions
5.5.4, 6.6.11, 6.8.11 and 6.8.14 of
[
BS15
]
,
this category is canonically equivalent to the one defined by Ekedahl
if
X
satisfies condition (A) or (B) of
[
BS15
]
5.5.1.

2
2

2

Technically, Ekedahl only defines the category
\Dbc(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})
for
a finite extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
, so to be precise, we should say
that the category
\Dbc(X,E)
of Bhatt-Scholze is canonically
equivalent to the inverse
2
-limit over all finite subextensions of

E
of the tensor product over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
of
E
and of the category of
constructible
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-complexes defined
by Ekedahl.

This point of view is conceptually simpler
and has the advantage that direct images, tensor
products and internal Homs are the restriction of actual derived functors
on the categories
\D(X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)
.

The six operations on the categories
\Dbc(X,E)

(direct and inverses images, direct images with
proper support, exceptional inverse images, derived tensor products and
derived internal Homs) are constructed in sections 6.7 and 6.8 of

[
BS15
]
. Suppose that we are given a dimension function
\delta
on
X

(see Définition XVII.2.1.1 of
[
ILO14
]
), and let
K_{X}
be the corresponding
dualizing complex on
X
.
By this, we mean a potential dualizing complex on
X
for the dimension
function
\delta

(see Définition XVII.2.1.2 of
[
ILO14
]
); this is known to be unique
up to unique isomorphism (Théorème XVII.5.1.1 of
[
ILO14
]
) and
to be a dualizing complex (Théorème XVII.6.1.1 of
[
ILO14
]
).
We then denote by
D_{X}=\Homf_{X}(.,K_{X})
the duality functor defined
by
K_{X}
; it satisfies all the usual properties, see Lemma 6.7.20 of
[
BS15
]
.

The category
\Dbc(X,E)
has a canonical t-structure,
whose heart is the category
\Sh_{c}(X,E)
of
constructible sheaves (this is automatic if we use definition
6.8.8 of
[
BS15
]
for
\Dbc(X,E)
).
This category has
a full abelian subcategory stable by extensions
\mathcal{Loc}(X,E)
,
the category of lisse sheaves (or locally constant sheaves, or local
systems), see definition 6.8.3 of
[
BS15
]
.
We will only use
the category
\mathcal{Loc}(X,E)
if
X
is connected regular;
in that case (and more generally if

X
is geometrically unibranch), this category is equivalent
to the category of continuous representations of the étale
fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(X)

of
X
on finite-dimensional
E
-vector spaces (see
lemmas 7.4.7 and 7.4.10 and remark 7.4.8 of
[
BS15
]
; the equivalence
is given by taking stalks at a geometric point of
X
).
In particular, if
X
is smooth of relative dimension
d
over
a field
k
and if we use the dimension function
\delta:x\longmapsto\dim(\overline{\{x\}})

(see the beginning of section
2.2
), then
for every
\mathcal{L}\in\Ob(\mathcal{Loc}(X,E))
corresponding
to a representation of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(X)
, if we denote
by
\mathcal{L}^{\vee}
the lisse sheaf corresponding to the dual representation,
then
D_{X}(\mathcal{L})\simeq\mathcal{L}^{\vee}(-d)[-2d]
. Indeed, we have
K_{X}=\underline{E}_{X}(-d)[-2d]
, hence
\mathrm{H}^{0}\Homf_{X}(\mathcal{L},K_{X})=\mathcal{L}^{\vee}(-d)[-2d]
, and all
the
\mathrm{H}^{i}\Homf_{X}(\mathcal{L},K_{X})
for
i\geq 1
vanish by
exercise III.1.31 in
[
Mil80
]
(and lemma 6.7.13 of
[
BS15
]
).

2.2
Perverse sheaves

In this section, we assume that
X
satisfies the conditions
of Corollaire XIV.2.4.4 of
[
ILO14
]
(for example,
X
is of finite
type over
\mathbb{Z}
or over a field)
and we
fix the dimension function
\delta
on
X

defined by
\delta(x)=\dim(\overline{\{x\}})
.
As explained
in
2.1
, it determines a dualizing complex
K_{X}
and a duality
functor
D_{X}
.
We define two full subcategories
{}^{p}\D^{\leq 0}
and
Dp^{\geq 0}

of
\Dbc(X,E)
by the
following formulas:

{}^{p}\D^{\leq 0}=\{K\in\Ob\Dbc(X,E)|\forall x\in X,\ \forall i>-\delta(x),\ \mathrm{H}^{i}(i_{x}^{*}K)=0\}

and

{}^{p}\D^{\geq 0}=\{K\in\Ob\Dbc(X,E)|\forall x\in X,\ \forall i<-\delta(x),\ \mathrm{H}^{i}(i_{x}^{!}K)=0\},

where, for every point
x
of
X
(not necessarily closed),
we denote the inclusion
x\longrightarrow X
by
i_{x}
.
This is a t-structure on
\Dbc(X,E)
for the same reasons as in
[
BBD82
]

2.2.9-2.2.19: We consider couples
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{L})
, where
where
\mathcal{S}
is a finite stratification
of
X
by locally closed connected regular subschemes,
and
\mathcal{L}
is the data, for each stratum
Z
of

\mathcal{S}
, of
a finite set
\mathcal{L}(Z)

of lisse sheaves on
Z
, such that condition (c) of
[
BBD82
]

2.2.9 is satisfied.
We denote by
\D_{(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{L})}(X,E)
the full
subcatgeory of
\Dbc(X,E)
whose objects are the complexes

K
such that, for each stratum
Z
of
\mathcal{S}
and each

i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathrm{H}^{i}K_{|Z}
is isomorphic to an element of
\mathcal{L}(Z)
.
The categories
({}^{p}\D^{\leq 0},{}^{p}\D^{\geq 0})
induces a t-structure on

\D_{(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{L})}(X,E)
by gluing, as in
[
BBD82
]
1.4.
Then we note that the category
\Dbc(X,E)
is the filtered inductive limit of
its subcategories
\D_{(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{L})}(X,E)
, and that the t-structures
are compatible thanks to the purity theorem (
[
Fuj02
]
or
XVI.3 of
[
ILO14
]
).

We will call the t-structure
({}^{p}\D^{\leq 0},{}^{p}\D^{\geq 0})
the

perverse t-structure
on
\Dbc(X,E)
, and denote its heart
by
\Perv(X,E)
. This is the category of
perverse sheaves
on
X

(with coefficients in
E
). We denote the associated cohomology
functor by
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}:\Dbc(X,E)\longrightarrow\Perv(X,E)
.

Let us list the exactness properties of the (derived) sheaf operations
for this t-structure.

Suppose that we have a flat morphism of finite type
X\longrightarrow S
.
The following
proposition is an immediate consequence of the definitions.

Proposition 2.2.1
.

Let
u:T\longrightarrow S
be an étale map
(resp. the inclusion of the generic point of
S
),
and consider the functor

u^{*}:\Dbc(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X\times_{S}T,E)
.

Then
u^{*}
(resp.
u^{*}[-\dim S]
)
is t-exact.

Then we recall the properties proved in sections 4.1 and 4.2 of

[
BBD82
]
.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a finite type morphism.
Then:

(i)

The functors
D_{X}
and
D_{Y}
are t-exact.

(ii)

If
f
is affine, then
f_{*}
is right t-exact and
f_{!}

is left t-exact.

(iii)

If the dimension of the fibers of
f
is
\leq d
, then

f_{*}
(resp.
f_{!}
, resp.
f^{*}
, resp.
f^{!}
) is of perverse
cohomological amplitude
\geq-d
(resp.
\leq d
, resp.

\leq d
, resp.
\geq-d
).

(iv)

If
f
is quasi-finite and affine, then
f_{*}
and
f_{!}

are t-exact.

(v)

If
f
is smooth of relative dimension
d
, then
f^{!}\simeq f^{*}[2d](d)
, and
f^{*}[d]
and
f^{!}[-d]
are t-exact.
In particular, if
f
is étale, then
f^{*}=f^{!}
is t-exact.

(vi)

The external tensor product
\boxtimes:\Dbc(X,E)\times\Dbc(Y,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X\times Y,E)
is t-exact.

(vii)

The Tate twist functor
K\longmapsto K(1)
is t-exact.

Remember that the external tensor product
of
K\in\Ob\Dbc(X,E)
and
L\in\Dbc(Y,E)
is the object

K\boxtimes L
of
\Dbc(X\times Y,E)
defined by

K\boxtimes L=(pr_{X}^{*}K)\otimes(pr_{Y}^{*}L),

where
pr_{X}:X\times Y\longrightarrow X
and
pr_{Y}:X\times Y\longrightarrow Y
are the two projections
(see SGA 5 III 1.5, where
K\boxtimes L
is denoted

K\otimes_{\Spec k}L
).

Proof.

For (i), note that, by Theorem 6.3(iii) of
[
Eke90
]
,
for all
K,L\in\Ob\Dbc(X,E)
and every
x\in X
, we have a canonical isomorphism

i_{x}^{!}\Homf_{X}(K,L)\simeq\Homf_{x}(i_{x}^{*}K,i_{x}^{!}L).

Applying this to
L=K_{X}
and using the isomorphisms

i_{x}^{!}K_{X}\simeq E(\delta(x))[2\delta(x)]
that are part of the
definition of a potential dualizing complex (see Définition
XVII.2.1.2 of
[
ILO14
]
), we see that

K\in\Ob(Dp^{\leq 0})
if and only if
D_{X}(K)\in\Ob({}^{p}\D^{\geq 0})
.
As
D_{X}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\Dbc(X,E)}
, this also
implies that
K\in\Ob({}^{p}\D^{\geq 0})
if and only if
D_{X}(K)\in\Ob({}^{p}\D^{\leq 0})
,
and we are done.

Point (ii) is
proved exactly as Théorème 4.1.1 of
[
BBD82
]
, as soon as we have
the analogue of Artin’s vanishing theorem, which is proved in
Exposé XV of
[
ILO14
]
. Point (iii) is proved exactly as 4.2.4 of

[
BBD82
]
, and (iv) follows from (ii) and (iii). To prove point
(v), it suffices to prove the isomorphism
f^{!}\simeq f^{*}[2d](d)
; but
this is SGA 4 XVIII 3.2.5. Point (vi) is proved as in Proposition 4.2.8
of
[
BBD82
]
. Finally, point(vii) follows from (vi), because
K(1)
is the exterior
tensor product of the complex
K
on
X
and of the perverse sheaf
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(1)
on

\Spec k
.
∎

We define the intermediate extension functor as in
[
BBD82
]
: if

j:U\longrightarrow X
is a locally closed
immersion and
K
is an object of
\Perv(U,E)
, then

j_{!*}K=\mathrm{Im}({}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}j_{!}K\longrightarrow{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}j_{*}K).

The methods of section 4.3 of
[
BBD82
]
adapt immediately to our case and
give the following result:

Theorem 2.2.3
.

The category

\Perv(X,E)
is Artinian and Noetherian, that is, all its objects have
finite length. Moreover, the simple objects are of the form

j_{!*}L[d]
, where
j:Z\longrightarrow X
is a locally closed immersion, the
subscheme
Z
is connected regular of dimension
d
, and
L
is a lisse
sheaf on
Z
corresponding to an irreducible representation
of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(Z)
(we call such a
L
a
simple
lisse sheaf).

2.3
Perverse t-structures and the realization functor

Fix schemes
X,Y
as in section
2.2
.
By Theorem
A.2.3
and Section
A.4
, we have a triangulated
realization functor

\mathrm{real}:\Db\Perv(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X,E)
extending the inclusion
\Perv(X,E)\subset\Dbc(X,E)
, and similarly for

\Dbc(Y,E)
.

The proof of the following proposition is explained
on page
A.4
in Section
A.4
.

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a finite type morphism.

(i)

If
f
is quasi-finite and affine, then we have
commutative diagrams
(up to
natural isomorphism)

\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(Y,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(Y,E)}

and

\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(Y,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(Y,E)}

(ii)

If
f
is smooth and of relative dimension
d
, then we have
a commutative diagram
(up to
natural isomorphism)

\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(Y,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}[d]}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(Y,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}[d]}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X,E)}

(iii)

We have
a commutative diagram
(up to
natural isomorphism)

\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X,E))^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X,E)^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{X}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X,E)}

We need one last compatibility.
Suppose that we have a flat morphism of finite type
X\longrightarrow S
.
If
T\longrightarrow S
is étale
(resp. the inclusion of the generic point of
S
),

u:X_{T}\longrightarrow X
is its base change to
X
and
u^{*}:\Dbc(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X_{T},E)

is the restriction functor, then
u^{*}
(resp.
u^{*}[-\dim S]
) is
t-exact by Proposition
2.2.1
. The following result
is proved exactly as Proposition
2.3.1
(see page
A.4
in Section
A.4
).

Proposition 2.3.2
.

In the situation above, we have
a commutative
diagram (up to
natural isomorphism)

\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X,E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}[a]}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Db(\Perv(X_{T},E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}[a]}
\textstyle{\Dbc(X_{T},E)}

where
a=0
is
T\longrightarrow S
is étale and
a=-\dim S
if

T\longrightarrow S
is the inclusion of the generic point of
S
.

2.4
Horizontal constructible complexes

From now on, we fix
a field
k
of finite type over its prime field (in other words,

k
is the field of fractions of an integral scheme of finite type over

\mathbb{Z}
) and an algebraic extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
.
We will consider separated schemes of finite type over
k
and denote
by them by capital Roman letters such as
X
,
Y
,
U
etc.

We will recall some constructions and results of sections 1-3 of

[
Hub97
]
. In this article, Huber assumes that
k
is a number field, but,
as she notes herself in the remark after proposition 2.3, this is not
really necessary and all her constructions extend to the more general
situation considered here, either by Deligne’s generic constructibility
theorem (SGA 4 1/2 [Th. finitude]) or by Gabber’s finiteness results
(
[
ILO14
]
).

Let
\mathcal{U}
be the set (ordered by inclusion) of
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebras
A\subset k

that are regular and of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
and such that
k
is the
field of fractions of
A
.
By a theorem of Nagata
(see EGA IV 6.12.6), if
\mathcal{B}
is an integral scheme of finite type over

\mathbb{Z}
,
then the regular
locus of
\mathcal{B}
is open in
\mathcal{B}
. Hence
k=\varinjlim_{A\in\mathcal{U}}A
. So we are
in the situation of EGA IV 8 and can use the results of this reference.

If
A\in\mathcal{U}
, we say that a scheme over
\Spec A
is
horizontal

if it is flat and of finite type over
A
. Let
X
be a scheme over
k
.
We denote by
\mathcal{U}X
the category of triples
(A,\mathcal{X},u)
, where
A\in\mathcal{U}
,

\mathcal{X}
is a horizontal scheme over
A
and
u
is an isomorphism of
k
-schemes

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}k
; we will often omit
u
from the notation. A morphism

(A,\mathcal{X},u)\longrightarrow(A^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
is an inclusion
A\subset A^{\prime}
and an open embedding

f:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
such that
u^{\prime}=u\circ f
. Then we have a canonical
isomorphism (given by the entry
u
of the triples)

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varinjlim_{(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X}\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}k.

If
f:(A,\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow(A^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime})
is a morphism
in
\mathcal{U}X
, then it induces an exact functor

\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Dbc(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},E),

where the first functor is the restriction functor along the open embedding

\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

(See
[
Hub97
]
, Definition 1.2.)
Let
X
be a scheme over
k
.
We define the category
\Dbh(X,E)
by

\Dbh(X,E)=2-\varinjlim_{(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X}\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E).

We call this category the
category
of bounded constructible horizontal
E
-complexes
of sheaves on
X
.

Note that we could also define versions of these categories with coefficients
in
\mathcal{O}_{E}
(if
E
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
), as Huber does. But
we will only be interested in this article in the category
\Dbh(X,E)
.

As in the remark following Definition 1.2 of
[
Hub97
]
, we see that
these categories are triangulated and have a tautological
t-structure (induced by the tautological
t-structure on the categories
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)
), and that all the
properties of Theorem 6.3 of
[
Eke90
]
carry over.
The heart of the canonical t-structure will be denoted by
\Sh_{h}(X,E)
, and we will call
its objects

horizontal constructible sheaves
on
X
.

We denote by
\eta^{*}:\Dbh(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X,E)
the exact
functor induced
by the restriction functors
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}k,E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Dbc(X,E)
, for
(A,\mathcal{X},u)\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X
.

Proposition 2.4.2
.

(i)

The functor
\eta^{*}
is fully faithful on the heart of the
tautological t-structure.

(ii)

If

\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\Ob(\Sh_{h}(X,E))
, then

\eta^{*}:\Ext^{1}_{\Dbh(X,E)}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(X,E)}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{F},\eta^{*}\mathcal{G})

is injective.

Proof.

The first point is proposition 1.3 of
[
Hub97
]
.
We prove the second point.
Let
(A,\mathcal{X},u)\in\Ob(\mathcal{U}X)
such
that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
come from objects
K
and
L
of
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)
.
We use

u
to identify
X
and
\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}k
.
The constructible sheaves
\bigoplus_{i\not=0}\mathrm{H}^{i}K
and

\bigoplus_{i\not=0}\mathrm{H}^{i}L
on
\mathcal{X}
are supported on a closed subset disjoint
from
X
, so, after shrinking
\mathcal{X}
, we may assume that

K
and
L
are constructible sheaves on
\mathcal{X}
. By definition of

\Dbh(X,E)
, we have

\Ext^{1}_{\Dbh(X,E)}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})=\varinjlim_{A\subset A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}}\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)}(K_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}},L_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}}).

Let
A^{\prime}\supset A
be an element of
\mathcal{U}
. The category
\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)
is a full subcategory of
\D((\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime})_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)
, the
derived category of the category of
E
-modules on the proétale
site of
\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
, so the groups
\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)}

parametrize extensions in this category of
E
-modules (see section
3.2 of chapter III of Verdier’s book
[
Ver96
]
). But
\Sh_{c}(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)
is a Serre subcategory of the category of all sheaves of
E
-modules
(see proposition 6.8.11 of
[
BS15
]
), so

\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)}(K_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}},L_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}})

is the group of equivalence classes of extensions of

K_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}}

by
L_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}}
in
\Sh_{c}(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)
. We have a similar
statement for
\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(X,E)}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{F},\eta^{*}\mathcal{G})
.

Now let
c\in\Ext^{1}_{\Dbh(X,E)}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
, and suppose that its
image in
\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(X,E)}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{F},\eta^{*}\mathcal{G})
is
0
. There exists
an element
A^{\prime}\supset A
of
\mathcal{U}
and an extension

0\longrightarrow L_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}}\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow K_{|\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}}\longrightarrow 0

in
\Sh_{c}(\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},E)
whose class is
c
. The hypothesis
on
c
says that the restriction of this extension to
X

is split. But, by point (i), this implies that there exists an element

A^{\prime\prime}\supset A^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{U}
such that the restriction of the extension
to
\mathcal{X}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime\prime}
already splits, which means that
c=0
.
∎

2.5
Horizontal perverse sheaves

In this section, we define the perverse t-structure on
\Dbh(X,E)
.
Note that, by Proposition 1.4 of Giral’s article
[
Gir81
]
,
all the rings
A
in
\mathcal{U}
have the same Krull dimension
c
, which is
the transcendence degree of
k
over its prime field if
k
is of positive characteristic, and

1
plus this transcendence degree if
k
is of characteristic
0
.

If
(A,\mathcal{X},u)\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X
, then we consider the perverse t-structure
on
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)
defined in section
2.2
.
Then the functor
u^{*}[-c]:\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X,E)
is t-exact
by Proposition
2.2.1
.
Also, for every morphism

f:(A,\mathcal{X},u)\longrightarrow(A^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{U}X
, the restriction functor

f^{*}:\Dbc(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)
is t-exact by the same proposition.
By taking the limit of the shift by
c
of the t-structures on the

\Dbc(\mathcal{X},E)
,
we get a t-structure on
\Dbh(X,E)
such that
\eta^{*}:\Dbh(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X,E)
is t-exact. We denote the heart of this t-structure by

\Perv_{h}(X,E)
and call it the category of
horizontal perverse sheaves

on
X
. We still denote the perverse cohomology functors by
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}:\Dbh(X,E)\longrightarrow\Perv_{h}(X,E)
.
We also have a realization functor
\mathrm{real}:\Db\Perv_{h}(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbh(X,E)

extending the inclusion
\Perv_{h}(X,E)\subset\Dbh(X,E)
,
by Theorem
A.2.3
and Section
A.4
.

Remark 2.5.1
.

This is not Huber’s construction.
Let us recall her construction
and compare it with ours.
Let
A\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\mathcal{X}
be a horizontal scheme over
A
. As in
[
Hub97
]
2.1,
we say that a stratification of
\mathcal{X}
is
horizontal

if all its strata are
smooth over
A
.
Suppose that
E/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
is finite.
If
S
is a horizontal stratification of
\mathcal{X}
and

L
is the data of a set of irreducible lisse
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-sheaves on every stratum
of
S
satisfying condition (c) of
[
Hub97
]
2.2, we get as in Definition 2.2
and Lemma 2.4 of
[
Hub97
]
a full subcategory
D^{b}_{(S,L)}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{E})
of

(S,L)
-constructible objects in
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{E})
, and it has a self-dual
perverse t-structure, whose heart we will denote by
\Perv_{(S,L)}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{E})
.
Because the strata
S
are smooth over
\Spec A
, lisse sheaves on them are
perverse for our t-structure on
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{E})
when placed
in degree
-\dim(A)=-c
, so Huber’s t-structure is the shift
by
c
of the one
induced by our perverse t-structure on
\Dbc(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{E})
.

By Proposition 2.3 of
[
Hub97
]
,
the category
\Dbh(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})
is the 2-colimit
of the categories
D^{b}_{(S,L)}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{E})
over all
(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X
and
couples
(S,L)
as before, and by Theorem 2.5 of
[
Hub97
]
, the perverse
t-structure goes to the limit and induces a t-structure on
\Dbh(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})
.
This is the t-structure that is used in
[
Hub97
]
, and, by the observation
of the previous paragraph, it coincides with the one that we defined
at the beginning of this section.

Huber’s definition has the advantage that we can apply Deligne’s generic
base change theorem (from SGA 4 1/2 [Th. finitude]) to deduce statements
for horizontal perverse sheaves from statements for perverse
sheaves on schemes over finite fields proved in sections 4 and 5 of

[
BBD82
]
.

For example, we see as in
[
Hub97
]
2.7 that
the six operations have the usual exactness properties
with respect to the perverse t-structure (which means the properties of

[
BBD82
]
4.1 and 4.2), that the category
\Perv_{h}(X,E)
is
Artinian and Noetherian, and that we have the same description of its simple
objects as in Theorem 4.3.1 of
[
BBD82
]
.

3
3

3

But we could also have proved all these statements from our
definition.

The following result is a slight generalization of the first part of
Lemma 2.12 of
[
Hub97
]
.

Proposition 2.5.2
.

1.

The functor
\eta^{*}:\Perv_{h}(X,E)\longrightarrow\Perv(X,E)
is fully
faithful, and its essential image is the full category of perverse sheaves
on
X
that extend to a constructible complex on some
\mathcal{X}
, for

(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X
.

2.

For every
K,L\in\Ob\Perv(X,E)
, the morphism

\Ext^{1}_{\Dbh(X,E)}(K,L)\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\Dbc(X,E)}(\eta^{*}K,\eta^{*}L)

induced by
\eta
is injective.

Proof.

If the category where we take the
\Ext^{i}
is
clear from context, we omit it in this proof. Also, we omit the
coefficients
E
in the notation.

We prove both points by Noetherian induction on
X
. If
\dim X=0
,
then the perverse
t
-structure on
\Dbc(X,E)
is the usual t-structure, so both points follow from Proposition

2.4.2
(which is an easy consequence of Proposition
1.3 of
[
Hub97
]
).

Suppose that
\dim X>0
, and let
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{h}(X)
. Lemma 2.12
of
[
Hub97
]
says that the map
\Hom(K,L)\longrightarrow\Hom(\eta^{*}K,\eta^{*}L)
is injective, and we want to show
that it is also surjective. We show this by induction on the sum of the
lengths of
K
and
L
.

Suppose first that
K
and
L
are both simple. Then we have smooth connected
locally closed subschemes
k_{1}:Y_{1}\longrightarrow X
and
k_{2}:Y_{2}\longrightarrow X
and
horizontal
locally constant sheaves
\mathcal{L}_{1}
on
Y_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
on
Y_{2}

such that
K=k_{1!*}\mathcal{L}_{1}[\dim Y_{1}]
and
L=k_{2!*}\mathcal{L}_{2}[\dim Y_{2}]
.
We have
\Hom(K,L)=0
if
K\not\simeq L
, and

\Hom(K,K)=\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{1})
.
In particular, by
Proposition 1.3 of
[
Hub97
]
,
\eta^{*}K
and
\eta^{*}L
are also simple, and

\Hom(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom(\eta^{*}K,\eta^{*}L)
, proving the first point.

We prove the second point.
Let
Z=\overline{Y}_{1}\cap\overline{Y}_{2}
, and denote by
i:Z\longrightarrow X
and

j:X-Z\longrightarrow X
the inclusions. We have an exact triangle

R\Hom(i^{*}K,i^{!}L)\longrightarrow R\Hom(K,L)\longrightarrow R\Hom(j^{*}K,j^{*}L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}.

As
j^{*}K
and
j^{*}L
are perverse with disjoint supports on
X-Z
,

R\Hom(j^{*}K,j^{*}L)=0
, so we get isomorphisms
\Ext^{i}(i^{*}K,i^{!}L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Ext^{i}(K,L)
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. We have a similar result for
\eta^{*}K

and
\eta^{*}L
.

If
\overline{Y}_{1}
is not contained is
\overline{Y}_{2}
, then
Z
is a proper
closed subset of
\overline{Y}_{1}
, so
i^{*}K
and
i^{*}\eta^{*}K
are concentrated
in perverse degree
\leq-1
. As
i^{!}L
and
i^{!}\eta^{*}L
are concentrated in
perverse degree
\geq 0
, we get

\Ext^{1}(K,L)=\Hom({}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}K,{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{!}L)

and

\Ext^{1}(\eta^{*}K,\eta^{*}L)=\Hom({}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}\eta^{*}K,{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{!}\eta^{*}L),

so the second point follows from the induction hypothesis applied to

Z
.

If
\overline{Y}_{2}
is not contained is
\overline{Y}_{1}
, then
Z
is a proper
closed subset of
\overline{Y}_{2}
, so
i^{!}L
and
i^{!}\eta^{*}L
are concentrated
in perverse degree
\geq 1
. As
i^{*}K
and
i^{*}\eta^{*}K
are concentrated in
perverse degree
\leq 0
, we get

\Ext^{1}(K,L)=\Hom({}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{*}K,{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{1}i^{!}L)

and

\Ext^{1}(\eta^{*}K,\eta^{*}L)=\Hom({}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{*}\eta^{*}K,{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{1}i^{!}\eta^{*}L),

so the second point again follows from the induction hypothesis applied to

Z
.

Finally, suppose that
\overline{Y}_{1}=\overline{Y}_{2}
. Then
i^{*}K
and

i^{!}L
are perverse and simple, and we may assume that
Y_{1}=Y_{2}
. Let
b
be the
inclusion of the open subscheme
Y_{1}
of
Z
, and
a
be the inclusion
of its complement. As before, we have an exact triangle

R\Hom(a^{*}i^{*}K,a^{!}i^{!}L)\longrightarrow R\Hom(i^{*}K,i^{!}L)\longrightarrow R\Hom(b^{*}i^{*}K,b^{*}i^{!}L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}.

As
i^{*}K
and
i^{!}L
are simple of support
Z
, we know that

a^{*}i^{*}K
is concentrated in perverse degree
\leq-1
and that

a^{!}i^{!}L
is concentrated in perverse degree
\geq 1
, so we get an
injective map

\Ext^{1}(i^{*}K,i^{!}L)\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}(b^{*}i^{*}K,b^{*}i^{!}L)=\Ext^{1}_{\Dbh(X)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}).

We have a similar calculation for
i^{*}\eta^{*}K
and
i^{!}\eta^{*}L
, and
so the second point follows from Proposition
2.4.2
.
This finishes the proof in the case where
K
and
L
are both simple.

Now suppose that we have an exact sequence
0\longrightarrow K_{1}\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow K_{2}\longrightarrow 0
,
and that we know the result for the pairs
(K_{1},L)
and
(K_{2},L)
. We show it
for
(K,L)
. Write
K^{\prime}=\eta^{*}K
,
L^{\prime}=\eta^{*}L
etc.
We have a commutative diagram with exact rows

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(K_{2},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{\Hom(K,L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(K_{1},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}(K_{2},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}(K,L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}(K_{1},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(K^{\prime}_{2},L^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(K^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}(K^{\prime}_{2},L^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}(K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}(K^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime})}

so both points follow from the five lemma.

The case where we have an exact sequence
0\longrightarrow L_{1}\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow L_{2}\longrightarrow 0
such
that the result is known for
(K,L_{1})
and
(K,L_{2})
is treated in the
same way.
∎

2.6
Mixed perverse sheaves

The key observation in order to define weights in our setting, if
A\in\mathcal{U}
, then, as
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra of
finite type, the residue fields of closed points of
\Spec A
are finite, so
we can use the theory of
[
BBD82
]
chapter 5 as in section 3 of
[
Hub97
]

to define categories
\Dbm(X,E)
of mixed
horizontal complexes. Once we have defined what it means for a horizontal constructible
sheaf to be punctually pure of a certain weight, the definition
proceeds as in
[
BBD82
]
5.1.5. If
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob\Sh_{h}(X,E)

and
w\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we say that
\mathcal{F}
is
punctually pure of weight
w
if there exists

(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\Ob\Sh_{c}(\mathcal{X},E)
a constructible sheaf extending
\mathcal{F}

such that, for every closed point
x
of
\Spec A
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{|\mathcal{X}_{x}}
is punctually
pure of weight
w
in the sense of
[
BBD82
]
5.1.5 (that is, of
Deligne’s
[
Del80
]
). We say that
\mathcal{F}
is
mixed
if it has a filtration
whose graded pieces are punctually pure of some weight.

We denote by
\Dbm(X,E)
the full subcategory of
mixed complexes

in
\Dbh(X,E)
; the objects of
\Dbm(X,E)
are complexes

K
such that all the (usual) cohomology sheaves of
K
are
mixed.

By Proposition 3.2 of
[
Hub97
]
, these subcategories
are stable by the 6 operations and inherit a perverse t-structure from

\Dbh(X,E)
. We denote the heart of this t-structure by

\Perv_{m}(X,E)
; it is a full subcategory of
\Perv_{h}(X,E)
,
stable by subquotients and extensions. All the compatibilities between
the six operations (and the intermediate extension functor) and weights
that are proved in
[
Del80
]
and
[
BBD82
]
chapter 5 remain true, see

[
Hub97
]
3.3-3.6. Also, the realization functor
\mathrm{real}:D^{b}\Perv_{h}(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbh(X,E)
restricts to a functor
\mathrm{real}:D^{b}\Perv_{m}(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbh(X,E)
, whose essential image is contained in
\Dbm(X,E)
by
definition of
\Dbm(X,E)
.

Let us introduce weight filtrations, following Definition 3.7 of
[
Hub97
]
.

Definition 2.6.1
.

Let
K\in\Ob\Perv_{m}(X,E)
. A
weight filtration
on

K
is a separated exhaustive ascending filtration
W
on
K
(in the abelian category

\Perv_{m}(X,E)
) such that
\Gra_{k}^{W}K
is pure of weight
k
for every

k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

As the abelian category
\Perv_{m}(X,E)
is Artinian and Noetherian, such
a filtration is automatically finite. Note also that morphisms in

\Perv_{m}(X,E)
are strictly compatible with weight filtrations (Lemma
of 3.8
[
Hub97
]
), so in particular a weight filtration is unique if it exists.

Definition 2.6.2
.

Let
\Perv_{mf}(X,E)
be the full subcategory of

\Perv_{m}(X,E)
whose objects are mixed horizontal perverse sheaves
admitting a weight filtration.

This subcategory is clearly stable by subquotients in
\Perv_{m}(X,E)
, but
it is not stable by extensions (even if
X=\Spec k
),
see the warning before Proposition 3.4 of
[
Hub97
]
.

Finally, the following conservativity result will be very useful.

Proposition 2.6.3
.

(i)

The functor
\eta^{*}:\Dbh(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbc(X,E)
is conservative.

(ii)

The realization functor
\Db\Perv_{h}(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbh(X,E)
is conservative.

(iii)

The obvious functor
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X,E)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{h}(X,E)
is conservative.

Proof.

In all three cases, we have t-structures on the source and target for which the functors
are t-exact and such that the family of cohomology functors for the t-structure is conservative
(the perverse t-structure on
\Dbh(X,E)
and
\Dbc(X,E)
, and the canonical t-structure on the
derived categories). So it suffices to check that the functors on the hearts are conservative.
But these functors are all faithful and exact (in fact, they are all fully faithful), so they are
conservative.
∎

3
Main theorems

From now on, we will fix the algebraic extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
and
omit it in the notation.

3.1
Informal statement

Informally, the main theorems say that the sheaves operations
(
f_{*}
,
f^{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
,
\Homf
,
\otimes
, Poincaré-Verdier
duality, unipotent nearby and
vanishing cycles) lift to the categories
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
in
a way that is compatible with the realization functors
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbm(X)
, and that all the relations between these functors that are
true in the categories
\Dbm(X)
are still true in the categories

\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

A convenient way to say this is to use the formalism introduced
in Ayoub’s thesis
[
Ayo07
]
(and in his article
[
Ayo10
]
).
Then Theorem
3.2.4
says that the four operations

f_{*}
,
f^{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
exist and satisfy all the expected adjunctions
and compatibilities, and Theorem
3.2.12
asserts the existence
and properties of the derived internal
\Hom
s and derived tensor products.
The stability of the categories
\Perv_{mf}
under the perverse direct image functors
is proved in section

6.3
, and the
unipotent vanishing cycles are constructed in section

5.2
(see Corollary
6.3.3
).

3.2
Formal statement

We denote by
\mathbf{Sch}/k
the category of schemes over
k

(always assumed to be
separated of finite type, as before)
and by
\mathfrak{TR}
the
2
-category of triangulated
categories.

The notion of a formalism of the four operations (
f^{*}
,
f_{*}
,
f_{!}
,
f^{!}
)
has been axiomatized by Deligne, Voevodsky and Ayoub, under the name
of ‘‘foncteur croisé’’.

4
4

4

There are other approaches, but this particular one seems better
suited to our situation. For example, using derivators is complicated by the
fact that it is difficult to make sense of the notion of “perverse
sheaf over a diagram of schemes”, because inverse image functors typically
do not preserve perverse sheaves.

We will follow Ayoub’s presentation.

Definition 3.2.1
.

(See Definition 1.2.12 of
[
Ayo07
]
.)

5
5

5

Note that
we take the two categories
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
of this reference to be equal
to
\mathbf{Sch}/k
.

A
crossed functor
(“foncteur croisé”) on
\mathbf{Sch}/k
with values
in
\mathfrak{TR}
(relatively to the class of cartesian squares)
is a quadruple of
2
-functors
H=(H^{*},H_{*},H_{!},H^{!}):\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
, such
that :

(0)

for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, we have
H_{*}(X)=H_{!}(X)=H^{*}(X)=H^{!}(X)

(we denote this triangulated category by
H(X)
);

(1)

the functors
H_{*},H_{!}
are covariant, and the functors
H^{*},H^{!}

are contravariant;

(2)

the functor
H^{*}
is a global left adjoint of
H_{*}
;

(3)

the functor
H^{!}
is a global right adjoint of
H_{!}
;

together with the data of exchange structures of type
\swarrow
on the couples

(H_{*},H_{!})
and
(H^{*},H^{!})
(see Definition 1.2.1 of
[
Ayo07
]
), i.e., for
every cartesian square

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, we have morphisms of functors
H_{!}(f)\circ H_{*}(g^{\prime})\longrightarrow H_{*}(g)\circ H_{!}(f^{\prime})
and
H^{*}(g^{\prime})\circ H^{!}(f)\longrightarrow H^{!}(f^{\prime})\circ H^{*}(g)
compatible
with horizontal and vertical composition of squares.

This data is moreover required to satisfy the following condition :
For every cartesian square

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, the morphisms

H^{*}(g)\circ H_{!}(f)\longrightarrow H_{!}(f^{\prime})\circ H^{*}(g^{\prime})
and
H_{!}(f^{\prime})\circ H^{*}(g^{\prime})\longrightarrow H^{*}(g)\circ H_{!}(f)
formally constructed used the exchange structures and
adjunctions (see the beginning of
[
Ayo07
]
1.2.4) are isomorphisms
and inverses of each other; equivalently, we could required that the
morphisms

H^{!}(f)\circ H_{*}(g^{\prime})\longrightarrow H_{*}(g)\circ H^{!}(f^{\prime})
and
H_{*}(g)\circ H^{!}(f^{\prime})\longrightarrow H^{!}(f)\circ H_{*}(g^{\prime})
are isomorphisms
and inverses of each other.

Definition 3.2.2
.

(See Definition 3.1 and Theorem 3.4 of
[
Ayo10
]
.)
Suppose that we have two crossed functors
H_{1},H_{2}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
.
A
morphism of crossed functors

R:H_{1}\longrightarrow H_{2}
is the following data :

(1)

For every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, a triangulated functor

R_{X}:H_{1}(X)\longrightarrow H_{2}(X)
.

(2)

For every
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, invertible natural
transformations

\theta_{f}:H_{2}^{*}(f)\circ R_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R_{X}\circ H_{1}^{*}(f)

\gamma_{f}:R_{Y}\circ H_{1,*}(f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{2,*}(f)\circ R_{X}

\rho_{f}:H_{2,!}(f)\circ R_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R_{Y}\circ H_{2,!}(f)

\xi_{f}:R_{X}\circ H_{2}^{!}(f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{2}^{!}(f)\circ R_{Y}.

We require these transformations to satisfy the compatibility conditions
spelled out in section 3 of Ayoub’s paper
[
Ayo10
]
.

Example 3.2.3
.

For
a\in\{c,h,m\}
,
we have a crossed functor
H_{a}=(H_{a}^{*},H_{a,*},H_{a,!},H_{a}^{!}):\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

defined in the following way :

•

For every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
,

H_{a}^{*}(X)=H_{a,*}(X)=H_{a,!}=(X)=H_{a}^{!}(X)=\Dba(X).

•

For every
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, we have

H_{a}^{*}(f)=f^{*}
,
H_{a,*}(f)=f_{*}
,
H_{a,!}(f)=f_{!}
and
H_{a}^{!}(f)=f^{!}
.

Moreover, we have morphisms of crossed functors
H_{m}\longrightarrow H_{h}\longrightarrow H_{c}
.

Then our first main result is the following theorem.

Theorem 3.2.4
.

There exists a crossed functor

H_{mf}=(H_{mf}^{*},H_{mf,*},H_{mf,!},H_{mf}^{!}):\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
and a morphism
of crossed functors
R:H_{mf}\longrightarrow H_{m}
such that, for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
,

H_{mf}(X)=\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
and
R_{X}:H_{mf}(X)=\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow H_{m}(X)=\Dbm(X)
is the composition of the obvious functor
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{m}(X)
and of the realization functor of section
2.5
.

Moreover, the functor
R_{X}
is conservative for every
k
-scheme
X
, and
we have for every morphism
f
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k

a natural transformation
H_{mf,!}(f)\longrightarrow H_{mf,*}(f)
, which is an isomorphism
if
f
is proper.

To prove this, we will follow the same strategy as in chapter 1 of

[
Ayo07
]
and section 3 of
[
Ayo10
]
, and deduce the existence
of the crossed functor and of the natural transformation
f_{!}\longrightarrow f_{*}

from that of a stable homotopic
2
-functor (see Definition
3.2.5
).

We note that the conservativity of
R_{X}
follows immediately
from Proposition
2.6.3
, and then
the fact that
f_{!}\longrightarrow f_{*}
is
an isomorphism for
f
proper follows from the conservativity
of the functors
R_{X}
.

Definition 3.2.5
.

(See
[
Ayo07
]
1.4.1.)
Let
H^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
be a contravariant 2-functor. For
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
,
we write
H^{*}(X)=H(X)
, and for
f
a morphism of
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, we also
denote the
1
-functor
H^{*}(f)
by
f^{*}
. We assume that
H^{*}
is
strictly unital, i.e., for every morphism
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
,
the connection isomorphisms
(f\circ\mathrm{id}_{X})^{*}\simeq f^{*}
and
(\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ f)^{*}\simeq f^{*}
are the identity.

We say that
H^{*}
is a
stable homotopic
2
-functor
if it satisfies the
following conditions :

(1)

H(\varnothing)=0
.

(2)

For every
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, the functor
f^{*}:H(Y)\longrightarrow H(X)

admits a right adjoint
f_{*}
. Moreover, if
f
is a locally closed immersion,
then the counit
f^{*}f_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}_{H(X)}
is an isomorphism.

(3)

If
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is a smooth morphism in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, then the
functor
f^{*}
admits a left adjoint
f_{\sharp}
. Moreover, if we have
a cartesian square :

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

with
f
smooth, then the exchange morphism
f^{\prime}_{\sharp}{g^{\prime}}^{*}\longrightarrow g^{*}f_{\sharp}

(defined formally using the adjonctions, see
[
Ayo07
]
1.4.5) is an
isomorphism.

(4)

If
j:U\longrightarrow X
and
i:Z\longrightarrow X
are complementary open and
closed immersions in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, then the pair
(j^{*},i^{*})
is conservative.

(5)

If
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/R)
and
p:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{X}\longrightarrow X
is the canonical
projection, then the unit
\mathrm{id}_{X}\longrightarrow p_{*}p^{*}
is an isomorphism.

(6)

With the notation of (5), if
s:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{X}
is the zero section,
then
p_{\sharp}s_{*}:H(X)\longrightarrow H(X)
is an equivalence of categories.

Definition 3.2.6
.

(See Definition 3.1 of
[
Ayo10
]
.)
Let
H_{1}^{*},H_{2}^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
be two stable homotopic

2
-functors. A
morphism of stable homotopic
2
-functors

R:H_{1}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{2}^{*}
is the data of :

(1)

For every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, a triangulated functor

R_{X}:H_{1}(X)\longrightarrow H_{2}(X)
.

(2)

For every
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, an invertible natural
transformation

\theta_{f}:f^{*}\circ R_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R_{X}\circ f^{*}.

We require that this data satisfy the following compatibility conditions :

(A)

The natural transformations are compatible with the composition
of morphisms in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
.

(B)

If
f
is smooth, then the natural transformation
f_{\sharp}\circ R_{X}\longrightarrow R_{Y}\circ f_{\sharp}
(obtained using the adjonction and
\theta_{f}^{-1}
)
is invertible.

Example 3.2.7
.

The crossed functors of Example
3.2.3

define (by forgetting part of the data) three stable homotopic
2
-functors

H_{m}^{*}
,
H_{h}^{*}
and
H_{c}^{*}
, and morphisms
H_{m}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{h}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{c}^{*}
.

Theorem
3.2.4
now follows immediately from the following two results
(the first one is a consequence of several theorems of Ayoub and is also
used to construct the four operations on the triangulated categories
of Voevodsky motives, and the second one is the main technical result of
this paper).

Theorem 3.2.8
.

(i)

(See Scholie 1.4.2 of
[
Ayo07
]
.)
Let
H^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
be a stable homotopic
2
-functor. Then
H^{*}
extends
to a crossed functor
\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
.

(ii)

(See Theorems 3.4 and 3.7 of
[
Ayo10
]
.)
Let
H_{1}^{*},H_{2}^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
be two stable homotopic
2
-functors
and
R:H_{1}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{2}^{*}
be a morphism. Let
H_{1},H_{2}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
be crossed
functors extending
H_{1}^{*},H_{2}^{*}
as in (i). Then
R
extends to a morphism
of crossed functors from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
.

Theorem 3.2.9
.

There exists a stable homotopic
2
-functor

H_{mf}^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
and a morphism of stable homotopic
2
-functors

R:H_{mf}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{m}^{*}
such that, for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
,
H_{mf}(X)=\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
and
R_{X}:H_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow H_{m}(X)
is the same functor as in
Theorem
3.2.4
.

Proof.

The construction of the
2
-functor
H_{mf}^{*}
is given
in Corollary
7.2.4
. Let’s check that it is a stable
homotopic
2
-functor. Property (1) is obvious. The fact that
H_{mf}^{*}(f)

admits a right adjoint for every
f
follows from the definition
of
H_{mf}^{*}
as a global left adjoint, and the last part of property (2)
follows from Corollary
6.4.2
and Proposition
7.1.7
.
The fact that
f^{*}
admits a left adjoint for
f
smooth is proved in
Proposition
7.3.2
(iv), and the last part of property (3) as
well as properties (4) and (5)
follow from the conservativity of the realization functor.
Finally, let
Y
be a
k
-scheme, let
p:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{Y}\longrightarrow Y
be the canonical
projection and
s:Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{Y}
be the zero section. By Proposition

7.3.2
(v), we have a natural isomorphism
s_{!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}s_{*}
.
So we get a natural isomorphism

p_{\sharp}s_{*}=p_{!}[2](1)s_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}p_{!}s_{!}[2](1)\simeq(ps)_{!}[2](1)=\mathrm{id}[2](1),

which shows that
p_{\sharp}s_{*}:\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)
is an
equivalence of categories.
∎

Finally, we show the existence of tensor products and internal
\Hom
s
on the categories
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Definition 3.2.10
.

(i)

(See Definition 2.3.1 of
[
Ayo07
]
.)
A
unitary symmetric monoïdal
stable
homotopic
2
-functor is a stable homotopic

2
-functor
H^{*}
that takes its values in the
2
-category of symmetric
monoïdal unitary triangulated categories, that is, that associates to
every
X\in\Ob\mathbf{Sch}/k
a unitary symmetric monoïdal
category
(H(X),\otimes_{X},1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})
and such that :

(a)

For every morphism
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, the functor

f^{*}
is unitary monoïdal.

(b)

(Projection formula.)
If
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is smooth,
K\in\Ob H(Y)
and
L\in\Ob H(X)
,
then the functorial map

p:f_{\sharp}(f^{*}(K)\otimes_{Y}L)\longrightarrow K\otimes_{X}f_{\sharp}(L)

constructed in Proposition 2.1.97 of
[
Ayo07
]
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

(See Definition 3.2 of
[
Ayo10
]
.)
Let
H_{1}^{*}
and
H_{2}^{*}
be two
symmetric monoïdal unitary stable homotopic
2
-functors.
Then a morphism of
symmetric monoïdal unitary stable homotopic
2
-functors
from
H_{1}^{*}
to
H_{2}^{*}
is a morphism of stable homotopic
2
-functors

R:H_{1}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{2}^{*}
such that :

(a)

For every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, the functor
R_{X}
is monoïdal
unitary.

(b)

For every morphism of
k
-schemes
f
, the natural
transformation
\theta_{f}
is a morphism of monoïdal unitary
functors.

(iii)

(See Definition 2.3.50 of
[
Ayo07
]
.) If
H^{*}
is as in
(i), we say that
H^{*}
is
closed
if, for every
X\in\Ob\mathbf{Sch}/k
,
the symmetric monoïdal category
(H(X),\otimes_{X})
is closed; this
means that, for every object
K
of
H(X)
, the endofunctor
K\otimes_{X}\cdot

of
H(X)
admits a right adjoint, that will be denoted by
\Homf_{X}(K,\cdot)
.

Example 3.2.11
.

The stable homotopic
2
-functors
H_{m}^{*}
,
H_{h}^{*}
and
H_{c}^{*}

are all closed symmetric monoïdal unitary (for the derived tensor product),
and the morphisms
H_{m}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{h}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{c}^{*}
are
morphisms of symmetric monoïdal unitary stable homotopic
2
-functors.

Our last result is the following :

Theorem 3.2.12
.

There exists a structure of closed symmetric
monoïdal unitary stable homotopic
2
-functor on
H_{mf}^{*}
such that

R:H_{mf}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{m}^{*}
is a
morphism of symmetric monoïdal unitary stable homotopic
2
-functors.

Moreover, for every
k
-scheme
X
, the functorial map

R_{X}\Homf_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\cdot,\cdot)\longrightarrow\Homf_{\Dbm(X)}(R_{X}(\cdot),R_{X}(\cdot))

of
[
Ayo10
]
(3.1) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This theorem is proved in section
8
.
More precisely, the bifunctors
\otimes_{X}
and
\Homf
are constructed in
section
8
, and all their properties are proved there except
for condition (i)(b) of Definition
3.2.10
. But this last
condition follows from the fact that the functor
R_{X}
is conservative
(and that the analogous result is true in
\Dbm(X)
).
∎

4
Easy stabilities

The proof of Theorem
3.2.9
will require us to show that
the full subcategories
\Perv_{mf}(X)\subset\Perv_{m}(X)
are preserved by
a certain number of sheaf operations. Here we list the easier such results.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a morphism of
k
-schemes.

(i)

If
f
is smooth of relative dimension
d
, then the exact
functor
f^{*}[d]:\Perv_{m}(Y)\longrightarrow\Perv_{m}(X)
sends
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
to
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

(ii)

If
f
is proper, then, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
functor
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{k}f_{*}:\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{m}(Y)

sends
\Perv_{mf}(X)
to
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.

Proof.

Point (i) follows from the fact that
the functor
f^{*}[d]
is exact (see Proposition
2.2.2
)
and sends pure perverse sheaves to pure perverse sheaves (by
[
BBD82
]
5.1.14).
Point (ii) is Proposition 3.9 of Huber’s paper
[
Hub97
]
. (This proposition
is stated for
f
smooth, but its proof doesn’t use the smoothness of
f
.)
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
X,Y\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
.

(i)

The Poincaré-Verdier duality
functor
D_{X}:\Perv_{m}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\Perv_{m}(X)

sends
\Perv_{mf}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}
to
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

(ii)

The external tensor product functor
\boxtimes:\Perv_{m}(X)\times\Perv_{m}(Y)\longrightarrow\Perv_{m}(X\times Y)

sends
\Perv_{mf}(X)\times\Perv_{mf}(Y)
to
\Perv_{mf}(X\times Y)
.

(iii)

The Tate twist
functor
(1):\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{m}(X)
,
K\longmapsto K(1)

sends
\Perv_{mf}(X)
to
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact all these functors are exact (see Proposition
2.2.2
)
and send pure perverse sheaves to pure perverse sheaves (see
[
BBD82
]
5.1.14).
∎

In particular, by deriving trivially the functors above, we get :

(i)

For every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, an exact functor

D_{X}:\Db\Perv_{mf}^{\mathrm{op}}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
and an isomorphism
D_{X}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{id}
,
and also an exact functor
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
,
K\longmapsto K(1)
.

(ii)

For every
X,Y\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, an exact functor

\boxtimes:\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\times\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X\times Y)
,
satisfying the same properties of commutativity and associativity as the
external tensor product on the categories
\Dbc
.

Moreover, these functors
correspond to the usual ones on
\Dbm(X)
by the realization functor
(by Proposition
2.3.1
).

Note that, by Proposition 3.2.2 and Theorem 3.2.4 of
[
BBD82
]
, the

2
-functor
X\longmapsto\Perv(X)
is a stack for the étale topology on
X
.
We have the
following easy result :

Proposition 4.3
.

The categories
\Perv_{h}(U)
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(U)
, resp.
\Perv_{mf}(U)
)
define a substack of
X\longmapsto\Perv(X)
.

Proof.

As
\Perv_{h}(U)
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(U)
, resp.
\Perv_{mf}(U)
) is a full subcategory of
\Perv(U)
for
every
U
, we only need to show the following fact : If
K
is an
object of
\Perv(X)
and if there exists an étale cover

(u_{i}:U_{i}\longrightarrow X)_{i\in I}
of
X
such that
u_{i}^{*}K
is in

\Perv_{h}(U_{i})
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(U_{i})
, resp.

\Perv_{mf}(U_{i})
)
for every
i\in I
, then
K
is in
\Perv_{h}(X)
(resp.

\Perv_{m}(X)
, resp.
\Perv_{mf}(X)
).

We first treat the case of
\Perv_{h}
and
\Perv_{m}
.
We may assume that
I
is finite and that the
U_{i}
are
affine. For all
i,j\in I
, we denote the fiber product of
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
by

u_{ij}:U_{i}\times_{X}U_{j}\longrightarrow X
. Then, as
\Perv
is a stack, we have an exact sequence in

\Perv(X)
:

0\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}K\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}u_{ij*}u_{ij}^{*}K.

As the last two terms are in
\Perv_{h}(X)
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(X)
) by assumption, and as

\Perv_{h}(X)
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(X)
) is a full abelian
subcategory of
\Perv(X)
by Proposition

2.5.2
, the perverse sheaf
K
is also an object of
\Perv_{h}(X)
(resp.

\Perv_{m}(X)
).

We now treat the case of

\Perv_{mf}(X)
. Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. We need to construct a subobject
L

of
K
such that
L
is of weight
\leq a
and
K/L
is of weight
>a
.
For every
i\in I
, we set
L_{i}=W_{a}(u_{i}^{*}K)
, where
W
is the weight
filtration on
K_{i}
. By the uniqueness of the weight filtration, the
L_{i}

glue to a subobject
L
of
K
. As we can test weights on an étale
cover of
X
(for example by Theorem 5.2.1 and 5.1.14(iii)
of
[
BBD82
]
), this
L
satisfies the required conditions.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
i:Y\longrightarrow X
be a closed immersion, and let
K\in\Ob\Perv_{m}(Y)
.
Then
K
is in
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
if and only if
i_{*}K
is in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Proof.

If
K
is in
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, then
i_{*}K
is in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
by
Proposition
4.1
(ii).

Conversely, assume that
i_{*}K
is in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
. Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. We want to show that
there exists a subobject
K^{\prime}
of
K
(in
\Perv_{m}(Y)
) such that
K^{\prime}
is of weight
\leq a
and

K/K^{\prime}
is of weight
\geq a+1
. By the assumption, there exists a subobject
L^{\prime}\subset i_{*}K

(in
\Perv_{m}(X)
) such that
L^{\prime}
is of weight
\leq a
and
L^{\prime\prime}:=(i_{*}K)/L^{\prime}
is of weight
\geq a+1
.
Let
j
be the inclusion of the complement of
Y
in
X
. Then the functor
j^{*}
is t-exact, so,
applying
j^{*}
to the exact sequence
0\longrightarrow L^{\prime}\longrightarrow i_{*}K\longrightarrow L^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0
, we get an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow j^{*}L^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow j^{*}L^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0
of mixed perverse sheaves on
X-Y
. This implies that

j^{*}L^{\prime}=j^{*}L^{\prime\prime}=0
, so the adjunction morphisms
i_{*}i^{!}L^{\prime}\longrightarrow L^{\prime}\longrightarrow i_{*}i^{*}L^{\prime}
and

i_{*}i^{!}L^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow L^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow i_{*}i^{*}L^{\prime\prime}
are isomorphisms. In particular, the mixed complexes

i^{*}L^{\prime}=i^{!}L^{\prime}
and
i^{*}L^{\prime\prime}=i^{!}L^{\prime\prime}
are perverse. Let
K^{\prime}=i^{*}L^{\prime}
. We have just seen that
K^{\prime}
is perverse,
and the weights of
K^{\prime}
are
\leq a
(see the remark after Definition 3.3 of
[
Hub97
]
). Also,
we have an exact triangle
K^{\prime}=i^{*}L^{\prime}\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow i^{*}L^{\prime\prime}=i^{!}L^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
, which is actually an
exact sequence in
\Perv_{m}(Y)
, so the canonical map
K^{\prime}\longrightarrow K
is injective, and

K/K^{\prime}\simeq i^{!}L^{\prime\prime}
, which is of weight
\geq a+1

(by the same remark in
[
Hub97
]
).
∎

5
Beilinson’s construction of unipotent nearby cycles

In this section, we review Beilinson’s construction of the unipotent nearby
and vanishing cycles functors from
[
Beĭ87a
]
. There are two reasons
to do this:

(1)

We will want to define nearby cycles for horizontal
perverse sheaves, and to apply known theorems (about weights for example).
The easiest way to do this is to use Deligne’s generic base change theorem,
but this might cause technical problems if we use the original
construction of nearby cycles (from SGA 7 I and XIII), which involves
direct images by morphisms that are not of finite type.

(2)

We will need some of Beilinson’s auxiliary functors anyway
to construct a left adjoint of
i_{*}
for
i
a closed immersion.

All the proofs of the results in this section can be found in
[
Beĭ87a
]

(see also
[
Mor18
]
).

5.1
Unipotent nearby cycles

Fix a base field
k
, let
X
be a
k
-scheme, and let

f:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
be a morphism.
We write

\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathbb{A}^{1}-\{0\}
,

U=X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}X
and

Y=X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\{0\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}X
.

We have an exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\text{geom}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)\longrightarrow\Gal(\overline{k}/k)\longrightarrow 1,

which is split by the morphism coming from the unit section of

\mathbb{G}_{m}
. If
k
is of characteristic
0
, then

\pi_{1}^{\text{geom}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
; if
k
is of characteristic

p>0
, then
\pi_{1}^{\text{geom},(p)}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{(p)}(1)
. In both
cases, we get a projection
t_{\ell}:\pi_{1}^{\text{geom}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}(1)
. We also
denote by
\chi:\Gal(\overline{k}/k)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}

the
\ell
-adic cyclotomic character.

Let
\Psi_{f}:\Dbc(U)\longrightarrow\Dbc(Y_{\overline{k}})
and

\Phi_{f}:\Dbc(X)\longrightarrow\Dbc(Y_{\overline{k}})
be the nearby and vanishing cycles
functors defined in SGA 7 Exposé XVIII, shifted by
-1
so that they
will be t-exact for the perverse t-structure. (See
Corollary 4.5 of Illusie’s
[
Ill94
]
, and
note that the dimension function we use on
U
is shifted by

+1
when compared with Illusie’s dimension function.)
We denote by
T
a topological generator of
\pi_{1}^{\text{geom}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)
or

\pi_{1}^{\text{geom},(p)}(\mathbb{G}_{m})
(depending on the characteristic of
k
).
We have a functorial exact triangle

\Psi_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Psi_{f}\longrightarrow i^{*}j_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

There exists a functorial
T
-equivariant direct sum
decomposition
\Psi_{f}=\Psi_{f}^{u}\oplus\Psi_{f}^{nu}
such that, for every

K\in\Dbc(U)
,
T-1
acts nilpotently on
\Psi_{f}^{u}(K)
and invertibly
on
\Psi_{f}^{nu}(K)
.

In particular, the functorial exact triangle
\Psi_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Psi_{f}\longrightarrow i^{*}j_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
induces a functorial exact triangle

\Psi_{f}^{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Psi_{f}^{u}\longrightarrow i^{*}j_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
.

The functor
\Psi_{f}^{u}
is called the
unipotent nearby cycles functor
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that, for every
K\in\Dbc(U)
, there
exists a nonzero polynomial
P
(with coefficients in the coefficient field

E
that we are using for the categories
\Dbc
) such that
P(T)
acts by
0

on
\Psi_{f}(K)
. (The rest is standard linear algebra.)
As we know that
\Psi_{f}
sends
\Dbc(X)
to
\Dbc(Y_{\overline{k}})
(i.e.
preserves constructibility), this follows from the fact that,
for every
L\in\Dbc(Y_{\overline{k}})
, the ring of endomorphisms of
L

is finite-dimensional (over the same coefficient field
E
). To prove this
fact, we use induction on the dimension of
X
to reduce to the case where
the cohomology sheaves of
L
are local systems, and then it is trivial.
∎

Let
K\in\Dbc(U)
. Then
T:\Psi_{f}^{u}K\longrightarrow\Psi_{f}^{u}K
is unipotent, so there exists a unique nilpotent

N:\Psi_{f}^{u}K\longrightarrow\Psi_{f}^{u}K(-1)
such that
T=\exp(t_{\ell}(T)N)
on
\Psi_{f}^{u}K
.
The operator
N
is usually called the “logarithm of the unipotent part of
the monodromy”.
We get a functorial exact triangle
\Psi_{f}^{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Psi_{f}^{u}(-1)\longrightarrow i^{*}j_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
.

5.2
Beilinson’s construction

Now we introduce the unipotent local systems that are used in Beilinson’s
construction of
\Psi_{f}^{u}
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

For every
i\geq 0
, we define a
E
-local system

\mathcal{L}_{i}
on
\mathbb{G}_{m}
in the following way: the stalk

\mathcal{L}_{i,1}
of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
at
1\in\mathbb{G}_{m}(k)
is the
E
-vector
space
E^{i+1}
, on which an element
u\rtimes\sigma
of

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m},1)\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)\rtimes\Gal(\overline{k}/k)
acts by
\exp(t_{\ell}(u)N)\diag(1,\chi(\sigma)^{-1},\dots,\chi(\sigma)^{-i})
,
where

\diag(x_{0},\dots,x_{i})
is the
diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
x_{0},\dots,x_{i}

and
N
is the Jordan block

\begin{pmatrix}0&1&&0\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&1\\
0&&&0\end{pmatrix}
.

If
i\leq j
, we have an obvious injection
\alpha_{i,j}:\mathcal{L}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{j}
and
an obvious surjection
\beta_{j,i}:\mathcal{L}_{j}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i}(i-j)
.

Note that
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\vee}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{i}(i)
, so
(by the calculation at the end of section
2.1
)
we have
D_{U}(\mathcal{L}_{i})\simeq\mathcal{L}_{i}(i-1)[-2]
, and
D_{U}(\alpha_{i,j})

corresponds by this isomorphism to
\beta_{j,i}(j-1)[-2]
.

Notation 5.2.2
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a lisse sheaf on
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
K
is a perverse
sheaf on
U
, then the complex
K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}
is also perverse.
We denote it by
K\otimes\mathcal{L}
.

We start with the construction of
\Psi_{f}^{u}
.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

Let
K\in\Ob\Perv(U)
.

(i)

For every
a\in\mathbb{N}
, we
have a canonical isomorphism

i_{*}\Ker(N^{a+1},\Psi_{f}^{u}K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Ker(j_{!}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})\longrightarrow j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a}))=i_{*}{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a}).

In particular, if
a
is big enough, we get an isomorphism

i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{*}{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})
.

(ii)

For every
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N^{a+1}=0
on

\Psi_{f}^{u}K
,
the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{a,a+1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{a,a+1}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,a+1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,a+1}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}K(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})(-1)}

(iii)

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

N^{a+1}=N^{b+1}=0
on
\Psi_{f}^{u}K
. Then there is a
canonical isomorphism

\Ker(j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b})\longrightarrow j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b}))(-a-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Coker(j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\longrightarrow j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a}))

induced by the connecting map coming from the commutative
diagram with exact rows

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{a,a+b+1}}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a+b+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{b,a+b+1}}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b})(-a-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{a,a+b+1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a+b+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,a+b+1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b})(-a-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Moreover, the morphism

{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})\longrightarrow{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a+b+1})

induced by
\alpha_{a,a+b+1}
is zero.

Note in particular that we can use this construction to see

\Psi_{f}^{u}
as a functor from
\Perv(U)
to
\Perv(Y)
(and
not just to the category of
\Gal(\overline{k}/k)
-equivariant
objects in
\Perv(Y)
).

Corollary 5.2.4
.

For every
K\in\Perv(U)
, we have a canonical
isomorphism
D(\Psi_{f}^{u}K)\simeq\Psi_{f}^{u}(DK)(-1)
.

Corollary 5.2.5
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{N}
,
we define a functor
C_{a}^{\bullet}:\Perv(U)\longrightarrow C^{[0,1]}(\Perv(X))

(where the second category is the category of complexes concentrated in
degrees
0
and
1
) by

C_{a}^{\bullet}(K)=(j_{!}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})\longrightarrow j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})).

With the transition morphisms given by the
\alpha_{a,b}
, the family

(C_{a})_{a\geq 0}
becomes an inductive system of functors.

Then we have canonical isomorphisms

i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}\simeq\varinjlim_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(C_{a}^{\bullet})

and

0=\varinjlim_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{H}^{1}(C_{a}^{\bullet}).

Remark 5.2.6
.

If we use the Ind-category
\Ind(\Perv(X))
of
\Perv(X)
(see for example
Chapter 6 of Kashiwara and Schapira’s book

[
KS06
]
, and Theorem 8.6.5 of the same book for the fact
that this category is abelian),
then we can reformulate this corollary in the
following way:
We have a canonical isomorphism

i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varinjlim_{a\in\mathbb{N}}C_{a}^{\bullet}

of functors
\Perv(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Ind(\Perv(X))
. Note that, by Theorem 15.3.1 of
[
KS06
]
, the obvious
functor
\Db\Perv(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Ind(\Perv(X))
is fully faithful (and its
essential image is the full subcategory of complexes with all their cohomology objects
in
\Perv(X)
). So
\varinjlim_{a\in\mathbb{N}}C_{a}^{\bullet}
