factors through the category

\Db\Perv(X)
.

We now give the definition of the maximal extension functor.

Let
K\in\Ob\Perv(U)
.
For each
a\geq 1
, we have a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,a+1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,a+1}}
\textstyle{j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1)}

We write
\gamma_{a,a-1}:j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\longrightarrow j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1)

for the diagonal map in this diagram.

Proposition 5.2.7
.

(i)

For
a\in\mathbb{N}
big enough, the (injective) map

\Ker(\gamma_{a,a-1})\longrightarrow\Ker(\gamma_{a+1,a})
induced by

\alpha_{a,a+1}:j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\longrightarrow j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a+1})
is
an isomorphism.
We write
\Xi_{f}K
for the direct limit of the
\Ker(\gamma_{a,a-1})
.
This defines a left exact functor from
\Perv(U)
to
\Perv(X)
,
called the
maximal extension functor
.

Moreover, if
a
and
b
are big enough, then the map

\Coker(\gamma_{a,a-1})\longrightarrow\Coker(\gamma_{a+b,a+b-1})
induced by
\alpha_{a-1,a+b-1}(-1)
is zero. In particular, we have

\varinjlim_{a}\Coker(\gamma_{a-1,a})=0,

and so
\Xi_{f}
is exact.

(ii)

We have
a functorial isomorphism
D_{X}\circ\Xi_{f}\simeq\Xi_{f}\circ D_{U}
and two
functorial exact sequences

0\longrightarrow j_{!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Xi_{f}\longrightarrow i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}(-1)\longrightarrow 0

and

0\longrightarrow i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}\longrightarrow\Xi_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{*}\longrightarrow 0,

dual of each other,
in which the maps are the obvious ones. For example, in the first sequence,
the map
j_{!}K\longrightarrow\Xi_{f}K
is induced by the injection
\alpha_{0,a}:j_{!}K=j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{0})\longrightarrow j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})
, and the map

\Xi_{f}\longrightarrow i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}(-1)
is induced by the commutative square

\textstyle{j_{!}(\cdot\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{a,a-1}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{a,a-1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(\cdot\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{j_{!}(\cdot\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}(\cdot\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1)}

Remark 5.2.8
.

As in Remark
5.2.6
, we can deduce from
(i) of the proposition a natural isomorphism

\Xi_{f}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varinjlim_{a\in\mathbb{N}}(j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{a,a-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a-1})(-1))

in
\Db\Ind(\Perv(X))
.

The next functor that we construct is the
unipotent vanishing
cycles functor

\Phi_{f}^{u}
. It is not very hard to show that this functor
is isomorphic to the direct summand of the usual vanishing cycles
functor on which the monodromy acts unipotently, but we will not need
this, so we will just use the following proposition as the definition of

\Phi_{f}^{u}
.

Proposition 5.2.9
.

(i)

The complex of exact endofunctors of
\Perv(X)
defined by

j_{!}j^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha+\eta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Xi_{f}j^{*}\oplus\mathrm{id}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta-\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{*}j^{*}

in degrees
-1
,
0
and
1
, where
\eta:j_{!}j^{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}
is the counit of the
adjunction
(j_{!},j^{*})
and
\varepsilon:\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}
is the unit of the
adjunction
(j^{*},j_{*})
, has its cohomology concentrated in degree
0
and
with support in
Y
.

We define an exact functor
\Phi_{f}^{u}:\Perv(X)\longrightarrow\Perv(Y)
by setting

i_{*}\Phi_{f}^{u}
to be the
\mathrm{H}^{0}
of this complex.

(ii)

We denote by

\can:\Psi_{f}^{u}j^{*}K\longrightarrow\Phi_{f}^{u}K
the functorial map defined
by
i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}j^{*}K\longrightarrow\Xi_{f}j^{*}K
, and by

\mathrm{var}:\Phi_{f}^{u}K\longrightarrow\Psi_{f}^{u}j^{*}K(-1)
the functorial map defined by

\Xi_{f}j^{*}K\longrightarrow\Psi_{f}^{u}K(-1)
.

Then
\mathrm{var}\circ\can=N
and
\can(-1)\circ\mathrm{var}=N
.

(iii)

We
have a functorial isomorphism
D\circ\Phi_{f}^{u}\simeq\Phi_{f}^{u}\circ D
, and the
duality exchanges
\can
and
\mathrm{var}
.

(iv)

There are canonical isomorphisms
\Ker(\can)={}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}K

and
\Coker(\can)={}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{*}K
.

Dually, we have canonical isomorphisms
\Ker(\mathrm{var})={}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{!}K
and

\Coker(\mathrm{var})={}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{1}i^{!}K
.

Finally, we will need the functor that M. Saito calls
\Omega_{f}
.

Proposition 5.2.10
.

The functor
\beta+\varepsilon:\Xi_{f}j^{*}\oplus\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}
is
surjective. Its kernel
\Omega_{f}
is an exact endofunctor of
\Perv(X)
,
and we have functorial exact sequences

0\longrightarrow j_{!}j^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha-\eta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{f}\longrightarrow i_{*}\Phi_{f}^{u}\longrightarrow 0

and

0\longrightarrow i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}j^{*}\longrightarrow\Omega_{f}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow 0,

in which the unmarked maps are the obvious ones.

6
Nearby cycles and mixed perverse sheaves

The goal of this section is to show that the functor of unipotent nearby cycles
preserves the categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
and to deduce that these categories
are also preserved by the functors
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{k}f_{*}
, for every morphism

f
of
\mathbf{Sch}/k
. The main tool is Deligne’s weight-monodromy theorem
from
[
Del80
]
.

We also give an application to the direct image functor by
a closed immersion
i
, which then allows us to
construct the functor
i^{*}
on the categories
\Db\Perv_{mf}
.

6.1
Nearby cycles on horizontal perverse sheaves

We assume again that
k
is a field that is finitely generated over
its prime field. Let
X
be a
k
-scheme and
f:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}

be a morphism. We write

\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathbb{A}^{1}-\{0\}
,

U=X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}X
and

Y=X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\{0\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}X
.

In Section
5
, we have constructed exact functors

\Psi_{f}^{u}:\Perv(U)\longrightarrow\Perv(Y)
(see Proposition
5.1.1
),

\Phi_{f}^{u}:\Perv(X)\longrightarrow\Perv(Y)
(see Proposition
5.2.9
(i)),

\Xi_{f}:\Perv(U)\longrightarrow\Perv(X)
(see Proposition
5.2.3
(i)) and

\Omega_{f}:\Perv(X)\longrightarrow\Perv(X)
(see Proposition
5.2.10
).
The next proposition says that all these functors respect the full subcategories of
horizontal perverse sheaves (resp. mixed perverse sheaves).

Proposition 6.1.1
.

For
?\in\{h,m\}
,
the functor
\Psi_{f}^{u}
(resp.
\Phi_{f}^{u}
,
\Xi_{f}
,
\Omega_{f}
) sends the full
subcategory
\Perv_{?}(U)
(resp.
\Perv_{?}(X)
,
\Perv_{?}(U)
,
\Perv_{?}(X)
) of

\Perv(U)
(resp.
\Perv(X)
,
\Perv(U)
,
\Perv(X)
) to the full subcategory

\Perv_{?}(Y)
(resp.
\Perv_{?}(Y)
,
\Perv_{?}(X)
,
\Perv_{?}(X)
) of

\Perv(Y)
(resp.
\Perv(Y)
,
\Perv(X)
,
\Perv(X)
).

Proof.

Let
K\in\Perv_{h}(U)
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(U)
). As the lisse sheaves
\mathcal{L}_{a}
on
\Gr_{m}
of Definition
5.2.1

are horizontal and mixed, the perverse
sheaves
K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{a}
are horizontal (resp. mixed), so the perverse sheaves
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})
are all horizontal
(resp. mixed).
As
\Psi_{f}^{u}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}(K\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})
for
a
big enough (Proposition
5.2.3
(i)), we
deduce that
\Psi_{f}^{u}(K)
is in
\Perv_{h}(Y)
(resp.
\Perv_{m}(Y)
). The statement for
\Xi_{f}
,
\Phi_{f}^{u}
and
\Omega_{f}

follows immediately from their definition, once we know that the
\mathcal{L}_{a}
are horizontal and mixed.
∎

As only finite type schemes and
constructible complexes are involved in the definition of
\Psi_{f}^{u}

if we use the alternative definition given by Proposition
5.2.3
(i)), we
can use Deligne’s generic base change to compare our situation with the situation
over closed points of some ring
A\in\mathcal{U}
. We will see an
example of this in the next section.

6.2
The relative monodromy filtration

We recall the definition of the relative monodromy filtration, due
to Deligne.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

(See Propositions 1.6.1 and 1.6.13 of
[
Del80
]
.)
Let
K
be an object
in some abelian category, and suppose that we have a finite increasing
filtration
W
on
K
and a nilpotent endomorphism
N
of
K
. Then there
exists at most one finite increasing filtration
M
on
K
such that

N(M_{i})\subset M_{i-2}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and that, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}

and every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
the morphism
N^{k}
induces isomorphisms

\Gra_{i+k}^{M}\Gra_{i}^{W}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gra_{i-k}^{M}\Gra_{i}^{W}K.

Moreover, if
W
is trivial (that is, if there exists
i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\Gra_{i}^{W}K=K
), then the filtration
M
always exists.

The filtration
M
is called the
monodromy filtration on
K
relative
to the filtration
W
. If
W
is trivial, it is simply called the

monodromy filtration on
K
.

We will use the following theorem, which is a close relative
of Theorem 1.8.4 of Deligne’s Weil II paper
[
Del80
]
.

Theorem 6.2.2
.

Let
K\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(U)
, and let
W
be the weight filtration
on
K
. Then the monodromy filtration
M

on
\Psi^{u}_{f}K
relative to the filtration

\Psi^{u}_{f}W
exists, and
\Gra_{i}^{M}\Psi^{u}_{f}K
is pure of weight
i-1
for every

i\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
\Psi^{u}_{f}K
is an object of
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.

Before giving the proof of the theorem, we state and prove a lemma.

Lemma 6.2.3
.

In the situation of the theorem, suppose that
K
is
pure. Then the monodromy filtration
M
on
\Psi_{f}^{u}K
(which always exists)
is such that
\Gr_{i}^{M}\Psi_{f}^{u}K
is pure of weight
i-1
for every

i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
w
be the weight of
K
.
Let
(A,\mathcal{X},u)
be an object of
\mathcal{U}X
such that

K
comes by restriction from a shifted perverse sheaf
\mathcal{K}[-d]
on

\mathcal{X}
, where
d=\dim\Spec A
.
Fix
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N^{a+1}=0
on
\Psi_{f}^{u}K
.
After shrinking
\Spec A
and
\mathcal{X}
if necessary, we may assume that :

-

The morphism
f:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
extends to a morphism

F:\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}
.
We write

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}}\mathbb{G}_{A,m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle J}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}
and

\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{A}}\{0\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}
.

-

The lisse sheaves
\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{a+1}
all extend to

\mathbb{G}_{m,A}
. (In fact we can get all the
\mathcal{L}_{b}
as soon as we have

\mathcal{L}_{1}
, because they are
the symmetric powers of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
.)

-

For every closed point
x
of
\Spec A
, the restriction of

\mathcal{K}
to
\mathcal{X}_{x}
is still perverse, and it is pure of weight
w+d
.

-

The formation of the complexes

J_{!}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{b})
and
J_{*}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{b})
,
for
b\in\{a,a+1\}
,
is compatible with every base change
x\longrightarrow\Spec A
, where
x
is a closed
point of
\Spec A
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{L}
is any subquotient
of
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}I^{*}J_{*}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})
(in the category

\Perv(\mathcal{X},E)
), then its restrictions to the fibers of

\mathcal{X}
above all the closed points of
\Spec A
are still perverse.

Indeed, the first two points are standard, and the last two follow
from Deligne’s generic base change theorem (see SGA 4 1/2,
[Th. finitude], Théorème 1.9) and from the purity theorem.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}={}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-1}I^{*}J_{*}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{a})
, and
let
M
the monodromy filtration on
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
induced by
N
.
By the conditions above (and (i) of Proposition
5.2.3
),
for every closed point
x
of
\Spec A
, the restriction of

\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{X}_{x}
is a subobject of
\Psi_{f}^{u}\mathcal{K}_{x}
,
and the restriction of
M
is the monodromy filtration. The result
about the weights of the graded pieces of the monodromy filtration
over the spectrum of a finite field (such as
x
) is known
by Theorem 5.1.2 of Beilinson and Bernstein’s paper
[
BB93
]
(where it is
attributed to Gabber). So we get the conclusion by definition of the weights
on horizontal sheaves.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.2.2
.

We reason by induction on the length of the filtration
W
.
If
K
is pure (i.e., if
W
is trivial), then the conclusion of
the theorem is proved in Lemma
6.2.3
.

Now assume that
W
is of length
\geq 2
, and that we know the result
for every object of
\Perv_{mf}(U)
with a shorter weight filtration.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
W_{a}K=K
and
\Gra_{a}^{W}K\not=0
. By the induction
hypothesis, we know the theorem for
W_{a-1}K
and
\Gra_{a}^{W}K
.
Write
L=\Psi^{u}_{f}K
, and let
F
be the filtration
\Psi^{u}_{f}W
on
L
.
By Theorem 2.20 of Steenbrink and Zucker’s paper
[
SZ85
]
, the filtration

M
exists if and only if, for every
i\geq 1
, we have :

N^{i}(L)\cap F_{a-1}L(-i)\subset N^{i}(F_{a-1}L)+M_{a-i-1}F_{a-1}L(-i).

This is equivalent to saying that

(N^{i}(L)\cap F_{a-1}L(-i))/N^{i}(F_{a-1}L)\subset[M_{a-i-1}F_{a-1}L/(M_{a-i-1}F_{a-1}L\cap N^{i}(F_{a-1}L))](-i).

As the filtration
M
on
F_{a-1}L
is the weight filtration up to a shift,
the inclusion above is also equivalent to the fact that

(N^{i}(L)\cap F_{a-1}L(-i))/N^{i}(F_{a-1}L)
is of weight
\leq a+i-2
. Observe
that
(N^{i}(L)\cap F_{a-1}L(-i))/N^{i}(F_{a-1}L)
is the kernel of the
map
F_{a-1}L(-i)/N^{i}(F_{a-1}L)\longrightarrow L(-i)/N^{i}(L)
, so applying the snake lemma to
the commutative diagram with exact rows :

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{a-1}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N^{i}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N^{i}}
\textstyle{\Gra_{a}^{F}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N^{i}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{a-1}L(-i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L(-i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gra_{a}^{F}L(-i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

gives a surjection

\Ker(N^{i}:\Gra_{a}^{F}L\longrightarrow\Gra_{a}^{F}L(-i))\longrightarrow(N^{i}(L)\cap F_{a-1}L(-i))/N^{i}(F_{a-1}L).

But as
\Gra_{a}^{F}L=\Psi^{u}_{f}\Gra_{a}^{W}K
, we know by (1.6.4) of
[
Del80
]
that

\Ker(N^{i}:\Gra_{a}^{F}L\longrightarrow\Gra_{a}^{F}L(-i))
is of weight
\leq a+i-2

(or more correctly, we can deduce
this from the result we cited and Deligne’s generic base change theorem, as in the proof
of Lemma
6.2.1
),
and hence
all its quotients are. This proves the existence of the filtration
M

on
L
.

Finally, we prove that
\Gra_{i}^{M}L
is pure of weight
i-1
for every

i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The two properties defining
M
in Proposition

6.2.1
stay true if we intersect

M
with
F_{a-1}L
or take the quotient filtration in
\Gra_{a}^{F}L
, so this
gives the relative monodromy filtration on
F_{a-1}L
and
\Gra_{a}^{F}L

(by the
uniqueness statement). Hence we get exact sequences

0\longrightarrow\Gra_{i}^{M}F_{a-1}L\longrightarrow\Gra_{i}^{M}L\longrightarrow\Gra_{i}^{M}\Gra_{a}^{F}L\longrightarrow 0,

and so the fact that
\Gra_{i}^{M}L
is pure of weight
i-1
follows from the
induction hypothesis.
∎

6.3
Cohomological direct image functors and weights

Corollary 6.3.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a morphism of
k
-schemes. Then the
functors
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}f_{*},{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}f_{!}:\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{m}(Y)
send
\Perv_{mf}(X)
to
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.

Proof.

As Poincaré-Verdier duality exchanges
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}f_{*}
and

{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{-i}f_{!}
and preserves the categories
\Perv_{mf}
, it suffices to treat
the case of
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}f_{*}
.

By Nagata’s compactification theorem
(see for example Conrad’s paper
[
Con07
]
), we can write
f=gj
, with

j:X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
an open embedding and
g:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y
proper. After replacing
X^{\prime}
by
the blowup of

X^{\prime}-X
in
X^{\prime}
, we may assume that the ideal of
X^{\prime}-j(X)
is
invertible. Then
j
is affine, so
j_{*}
is t-exact, so
we have
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}f_{*}=({}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}g_{*})\circ j_{*}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Proposition

4.1
(ii), it suffices to prove the corollary for
j
.
By Proposition

4.3
, we may assume that
X^{\prime}
is affine, and hence that there
exists
h\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\prime})
generating the ideal of
X^{\prime}-j(X)
.

So we see that it is enough to prove the corollary in the following situation :
there exists
h:Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
such that
f=j
is the inclusion of
X:=h^{-1}(\mathbb{G}_{m})

in
Y
. Let
i:Y-X\longrightarrow X
be the inclusion of the complement. Let
K
be
an object of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
, and denote by
W
its weight filtration.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. We want to find a subobject
L
of
j_{*}K
such that

L
is of weight
\leq a
and
j_{*}K/L
is of weight
>a
. (This clearly
implies that
j_{*}K
has a weight filtration.)

If
W_{a}K=0
, then
K
is of
weight
>a
, so
j_{*}K
is of weight
>a
, and we take
L=0
.

If
W_{a}K=K
,
then
K
is of weight
\leq a
, so
j_{!*}K
is of weight
\leq a

by Corollary 5.4.3 of
[
BBD82
]
. So it is enough to find a subobject

L^{\prime}
of weight
\leq a
of
j_{*}K/j_{!*}K
such that
(j_{*}K/j_{!*}K)/L^{\prime}
is
of weight
>a
. But we know that
j_{*}K/j_{!*}K=i_{*}{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}K

(by (4.1.11.1) of
[
BBD82
]
), which
is a quotient of
i_{*}\Psi^{u}_{f}K(-1)
. As
\Psi^{u}_{f}K
has a weight filtration
by Theorem
6.2.2
, so does
j_{*}K/j_{!*}K
, and
we can find a
L^{\prime}
with the desired properties.

Suppose that
0\not=W_{a}K\not=K
, and let
K^{\prime}=W_{a}K
and
K^{\prime\prime}=K/W_{a}K
.
By the previous paragraph, there exists a subobject
L^{\prime}
of weight
\leq a

of
j_{*}K^{\prime}
such that
j_{*}K^{\prime}/L^{\prime}
is of weight
>a
. As
K^{\prime\prime}
is of weight

>a
, so is
j_{*}K^{\prime\prime}
. Using the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow j_{*}K^{\prime}\longrightarrow j_{*}K\longrightarrow j_{*}K^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0,

we see that
j_{*}K/L^{\prime}
is also of weight
>a
, so we can take
L=L^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.2
.

Let
j:U\longrightarrow X
be an affine open embedding.
Denote by

j^{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U)
and

j_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)
the
derived functors of the exact functors

j^{*}:\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(U)
and

j_{*}:\Perv_{mf}(U)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Then this derived functors
(j^{*},j_{*})
form a pair of adjoint functors.

Proof.

By Corollary 8.12 of
[
Shu11
]
, it suffices to prove that
the underived functors form a pair of adjoint functors. But, once we know
that both functors preserve the full subcategories
\Perv_{mf}\subset\Perv_{m}
,
this follows from the adjunction for the categories
\Perv_{m}
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.3
.

The exact functors
\Psi_{f}^{u}
,
\Phi_{f}^{u}
,
\Xi_{f}
and

\Omega_{f}
of section
5.2

preserve the full subcategories of mixed
perverse sheaves with weight filtrations.

Proof.

We already know the result for
\Psi_{f}^{u}
, by Theorem

6.2.2
.

Suppose that
K\in\Perv_{mf}(U)
. Then
K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{i}

is in
\Perv_{mf}(U)
for every
i\geq 0
. Indeed, if we denote by
W
the
weight filtration on
K
, then we get a weight filtration on
K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{i}

by setting

W_{a}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{i})=\sum_{0\leq j\leq i}(W_{a-2j}K)\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{j}.

By Corollary
6.3.1
, we see that

j_{!}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{i})
and
j_{*}(K\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{i})
are in
\Perv_{mf}(X)

for every
i\geq 0
. By definition of
\Xi_{f}
, this implies that

\Xi_{f}K\in\Perv_{mf}(X)
. The conclusion for
\Omega_{f}
then
follows from its construction in Proposition

5.2.10
.
Finally, by the construction in
Propositions
5.2.7
, the functor
i_{*}\Phi_{f}
is a subquotient of

\Xi_{f}j^{*}\oplus\mathrm{id}
. As
\Perv_{mf}(X)
is stable by subquotients in
\Perv_{m}(X)
, the
functor
i_{*}\Phi_{f}
sends
\Perv_{mf}(X)
to itself. By Lemma
4.4
,
this implies that
\Phi_{f}
sends
\Perv_{mf}(X)
to
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.
∎

6.4
Direct and inverse image by a closed immersion

Let
X
be a
k
-scheme and
Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}X

be a closed subscheme of
X
.
We denote by
D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)
the full subcategory of
D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)

whose objects are the complexes
K
such that the support of

\mathrm{H}^{i}K\in\Perv_{mf}(X)

is contained in
Y
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The exact functor
i_{*}:\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(X)
induces a functor
i_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)
,
whose image is obviously in contained in
D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Corollary 6.4.1
.

With notation as above, the functor

i_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)
is an equivalence of
categories.

We have a similar equivalence
\Dbm(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Dbm_{,Y}(X)
, where

\Dbm_{,Y}(X)
is the full subcategory of objects
K
of
\Dbm(X)
such that
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{i}K
is in

i_{*}\Perv_{m}(Y)
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Moreover, we can choose inverses
(i_{*})^{-1}

of these equivalences such that the
following diagram commutes :

\textstyle{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{R_{Y}}
\textstyle{D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i_{*})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{Y}}
\textstyle{\Dbm(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\Dbm_{,Y}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i_{*})^{-1}}
\textstyle{\Dbm(Y)}

where the functors
R_{X}
and
R_{Y}
are defined in Theorem
3.2.4
.

Proof.

We prove the first statement.
It suffices to prove that, for all
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(Y)
and every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the functor

i_{*}
induces an isomorphism
\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)}(K,L[n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(i_{*}K,i_{*}L[n])
. Note that both of these
\Hom
groups are
0
for

n<0
, so we only need to consider the case
n\geq 0
.
Fix
K\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(Y)
. The families of functors

(L\longmapsto\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)}(K,L[n]))_{n\geq 0}

and
(L\longmapsto\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(i_{*}K,i_{*}L[n]))_{n\geq 0}

are
\delta
-functors from
\Perv_{mf}(Y)

to the category of abelian groups (in the sense of Definition

[
Sta17
,
Tag 010Q
]
), and
i_{*}
induces a
morphism between these
\delta
-functors
(see Definition

[
Sta17
,
Tag 010R
]
). We want to show that
this morphism is an isomorphism.
We know that
i_{*}:\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(Y)}(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(i_{*}K,i_{*}L)
is
an isomorphism for every
L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(Y)
(because this is true in the categories

\Perv_{m}
).
Moreover, it follows easily from the Yoneda description of the extension groups in the
derived category (see Section 3.2 of Chapter III of Verdier’s book
[
Ver96
]
or
Lemma
[
Sta17
,
Tag 06XU
]
) that
the first of the two
\delta
-functors introduced above is effacable, i.e. satisfies the
hypothesis of Lemma
[
Sta17
,
Tag 010T
]
), and
hence is a universal
\delta
-functor
(see Definition

[
Sta17
,
Tag 010S
]
).
By Lemma
[
Sta17
,
Tag 010U
]

(and Lemma
[
Sta17
,
Tag 010T
]
again), it
suffices to prove that the second
\delta
-functor is also effacable. So we want to prove that,
for all
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, every
n\geq 1

and every
u\in\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)}(K,L[n])
, there exists an injective
morphism

L\longrightarrow L^{\prime}
in
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
such that the image of
u
in

\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)}(i_{*}K,i_{*}L^{\prime}[n])
is
0
.

Let
(U_{a})_{a\in A}
be a finite affine cover of
X
.
For every
a\in A
, we have a cartesian diagram of immersions

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y\cap U_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{a}}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}_{a}}
\textstyle{U_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{a}}

As
j^{\prime}_{\alpha}
and
j_{\alpha}
are affine, the functors
j^{\prime}_{\alpha*}
and
j_{\alpha*}
are
t-exact.
Let
L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(Y)
. By Corollary
6.3.1
,
the isomorphisms
i_{*}j^{\prime}_{a*}{j^{\prime}_{a}}^{*}L\simeq j_{a*}j_{a}^{*}i_{*}L
and

j_{a}^{*}i_{*}L\simeq i_{a*}{j^{\prime}_{a}}^{*}L
in

\Perv_{m}(X)
and
\Perv_{m}(U_{\alpha})

are isomorphisms of objects of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
and
\Perv_{mf}(U_{\alpha})
.
Using this and Corollary
6.3.2

we get for

K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(Y)
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
a canonical isomorphism

\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)}(i_{*}K,i_{*}j^{\prime}_{a*}{j^{\prime}_{a}}^{*}L[n])=\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U_{a})}(i_{a*}{j^{\prime}_{a}}^{*}K,i_{a*}{j^{\prime}_{a}}^{*}L[n]).

As we have an injective morphism
L\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{a\in A}j_{a*}j_{a}^{*}L
in
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
(by Corollary

6.3.1
again), this reduces the corollary to the case
where
X
is affine.

Now suppose that
X
is affine. By an easy induction on the number of generators
of the ideal of
Y
, we may assume that this ideal only has one generator, i.e.,
that there exists a function
f:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
such that
Y=X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\{0\}
.
The exact functor
\Phi_{f}^{u}:\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(Y)
induces a functor

\Phi_{f}^{u}:D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, and we have
\Phi_{f}^{u}\circ i_{*}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)}
. Let’s show that
i_{*}\circ\Phi_{f}^{u}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)}
, which will finish the proof.
By Proposition
5.2.10

and Corollary
6.3.3

we have two exact sequences
of exact endofunctors of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
:

0\longrightarrow j_{!}j^{*}\longrightarrow\Omega_{f}\longrightarrow i_{*}\Phi_{f}^{u}\longrightarrow 0

and

0\longrightarrow i_{*}\Psi_{f}^{u}j^{*}\longrightarrow\Omega_{f}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow 0,

where
j:X-Y\longrightarrow X
is the inclusion.
Note that the restriction of the functor
j^{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U)
to the full subcategory
D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)
is zero.
Hence the exact sequences above induces isomorphisms of endofunctors
of
D^{b}_{Y}\Perv_{mf}(X)
:

i_{*}\Phi_{f}^{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\Omega_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{id}.

The proof of the second equivalence of categories is similar, except that we don’t need
to use the Yoneda description to show that the
\Ext
groups in

\Dbm(Y)
define a
\delta
-functor.

The last statement of the Corollary
follows from the fact that we have isomorphisms

R_{Y}\circ\Phi_{f}^{u}\simeq\Phi_{f}^{u}\circ R_{X}

and

R_{X}\circ\Omega_{f}\simeq\Omega_{f}\circ R_{X}.

∎

Corollary 6.4.2
.

Let
i:X\longrightarrow Y
be a closed immersion. Denote by

i_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
the derived functor of
the exact functor
i_{*}:\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.

Then this functor
i_{*}
admits a left adjoint

i^{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)
, and the counit
i^{*}i_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}
of this
adjunction is an isomorphism.
Moreover, we have
an invertible natural transformation
\theta_{i}:i^{*}\circ R_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R_{X}\circ i^{*}
.

Finally, if
i^{\prime}:Y\longrightarrow Z
is another closed immersion, then the following
diagram is commutative :

\textstyle{R_{X}\circ i^{*}{i^{\prime}}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{i}}
\textstyle{i^{*}\circ R_{Y}\circ{i^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{i^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{i^{*}{i^{\prime}}^{*}\circ R_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{R_{X}\circ(i^{\prime}i)^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{i^{\prime}i}}
\textstyle{(i^{\prime}i)^{*}\circ R_{Z}}

where the vertical maps come from the composition isomorphisms
i^{\prime}_{*}i_{*}\simeq(i^{\prime}i)_{*}
and the uniqueness of the adjoint.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.4.1
, we have an equivalence of
categories
i_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D^{b}_{X}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, where

D^{b}_{X}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
is the full subcategory of
D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
whose
objects are complexes
K
such that the support of

H^{i}K\in\Perv_{mf}(Y)
is contained in
X

for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. So, to show that
i_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)

admits a left adjoint, it suffices to show that the inclusion
\alpha:D^{b}_{X}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
admits a left adjoint.
Let
j:Y-X\longrightarrow Y
be the inclusion. Then we have an exact triangle

j_{!}j^{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow i_{*}i^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
of endofunctors of
D^{b}_{m}(Y)
, and we can
make sense of the first two terms in
D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, so we will try to
construct the left adjoint of
\alpha
as their cone.

More precisely,
let
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be a finite open affine cover of
U:=Y-X
. For every

J\subset I
, we denote by
j_{J}:\bigcap_{i\in J}U_{i}\longrightarrow X
the inclusion.
As
X
is separated, all the finite intersections of
U_{i}
’s are affine,
so the morphism
j_{J}
is affine for every
J\subset I
.
If
K\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(X)
, we denote by
D^{\bullet}(K)
the complex
of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
defined by

D^{-r}(K)=\bigoplus_{|J|=r}j_{J!}j_{J}^{*}K
if
r\geq 1
,
D^{0}(K)=K
and
D^{r}(K)=0

if
r\geq 1
, where the maps

D^{-r-1}(K)\longrightarrow D^{-r}(K)
,
r\geq 0
,
are alternating sums of adjunction morphisms. Note that we have a morphism
of complexes
K\longrightarrow D^{\bullet}(K)
, where
K
is in degree
0
.
Also, there is a canonical morphism
D^{-1}(K)\longrightarrow j_{!}j^{*}K
, which induces an
isomorphism
D^{\leq-1}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{!}j^{*}K[1]
in
D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, so we get
a quasi-isomorphism
R_{Y}(D^{\bullet}(K))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{*}i^{*}K
in
D^{b}_{m}(Y)
.
In particular,
D^{\bullet}(K)
is in
D^{b}_{X}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.
Note that the construction of
D^{\bullet}(K)
is functorial in
K
, so
we can define a functor
\beta:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow D^{b}_{X}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
by sending
a complex
K
to the total complex of the double complex
D^{\bullet}(K)
.

Let’s show that
\beta
is left adjoint to
\alpha
.
For every complex
K
of objects of
\Perv_{mf}(Y)
,
the morphism of double complexes
K\longrightarrow D^{\bullet}(K)
induces a morphism

\varepsilon_{K}:K\longrightarrow\alpha\beta(K)
in
D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)
. If moreover
K
is in
D^{b}_{X}\Perv_{mf}(X)
,
then
K\longrightarrow D^{\bullet}(K)
is a quasi-isomorphism, so we get an
isomorphism
\eta_{K}:\beta\alpha(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}K
. Moreover, the morphism

\alpha(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{K}\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha\beta\alpha(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\eta_{K}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha(K)

is clearly the
identity of
\alpha(K)
. So we have constructed the unit and counit of the
adjunction, and shown that the counit is an isomorphism.

To construct the isomorphism
\theta_{i}
, we use the isomorphism

R_{Y}\circ V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{*}i^{*}\circ R_{X}
constructed above and the last statement
of Corollary
6.4.1
. The last statement is also easy
to check.
∎

7
Construction of the stable homotopic
2
-functor
H_{mf}

7.1
Direct images

If
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is a morphism of
k
-schemes, we write
{}^{0}f_{*}
for

{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}f_{*}
. Remember that if
f
is affine, then
f_{*}
is right
t-exact for the perverse t-structure by
[
BBD82
]
Theorem 4.1.1.

In this section, we want to prove the following result.

Proposition 7.1.1
.

There exists a
2
-functor
H_{mf,*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

with
H_{mf,*}(X)=D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)
for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
and
a natural transformation
R:H_{mf,*}\longrightarrow H_{m,*}
(with the notation
of Example
3.2.3
) such that :

(a)

for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, the functor
R_{X}:\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbm(X)
is the composition of the obvious functor

\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{m}(X)
and of the realization functor

\Db\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbm(X)
(see
Section
2.6
);

(b)

for every morphism

f:X\longrightarrow Y
, the natural transformation

\gamma_{f}:R_{Y}\circ H_{mf,*}(f)\longrightarrow H_{m,*}(f)\circ R_{X}
is an isomorphism.

The proof of the proposition will occupy most of this section.
The main ingredients are :

•

Beilinson’s version of the “basic lemma” that provides
f_{*}
-acyclic perverse
sheaves for
f:X\longrightarrow Y
an affine morphism.
(See Theorem
7.1.2
.)

•

The fact that the functors
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{k}f_{*}
(and in particular
{}^{0}f_{*}
)
preserve the categories
\Perv_{mf}
.
(See Corollary
6.3.1
.)

•

Čech resolutions for finite open affine coverings.

We first review Beilinson’s basic lemma.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a morphism of
k
-schemes, let
(a_{i}:U_{i}\longrightarrow X)_{i\in I}
be a finite
family of open affine subschemes of
X
and let
K\in\Ob\Perv(X)
. Then there exists
a finite family
(b_{j}:V_{j}\longrightarrow X)_{j\in J}
of open affine subschemes of
X
such that:

(i)

The canonical morphism
\bigoplus_{j\in J}b_{j!}b_{j}^{*}K\longrightarrow K
is surjective.

(ii)

For every
i\in I
, the object
\bigoplus_{j\in J}a_{i}^{*}b_{j!}b_{j}^{*}K
is
(f\circ a_{i})_{*}
-acyclic.

The theorem follows from the proof of Lemma 3.3 of
[
Beĭ87b
]
.

Note also that, as the categories
\Perv_{h}
,
\Perv_{m}
and
\Perv_{mf}
are stable by
the functors
j^{*}
and
j_{!}
for
j
an open affine embedding (by Corollary
6.3.1
for
\Perv_{mf}
), if
K
is
in one of these categories, so is
\bigoplus_{j\in J}b_{j!}b_{j}^{*}K
.

We now turn to the proof of Proposition
7.1.1
.

Following Section 3.4 of
[
Beĭ87b
]
, we explain how to reconstruct the functor
f_{*}

from
{}^{0}f_{*}
. More precisely, we want to apply Proposition
A.3.2
to

\mathcal{D}=\Dbm(X)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\Dbm(Y)
with the perverse t-structures,
\DF=\DFbm(X,E)
and
\DF^{\prime}=\DFbm(Y,E)

(see Section
2.6
) with the unique t-structures compatible with the perverse t-structures
(see Proposition
A.2.2
),
and
T=f_{*}
.

To check condition (a) of that proposition, remember that
\DFbm(X,E)
is by definition a full subcategory of
the triangulated category
\DFbh(X,E)
constructed in Section
2.4
; we have

\DFbh(X,E)=2-\varinjlim_{(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X}\DFbc(\mathcal{X},E)
, and each
\DFbc(\mathcal{X},E)
is a full
subcategory of
\DFb(\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)
. Of course, we have similar statements for
\DFbm(Y,E)
.
If we choose
(A,\mathcal{X})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}X
and
(A,\mathcal{Y})\in\Ob\mathcal{U}Y
such that
f
extends to an
A
-morphism

f:\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, then
Proposition
A.1.11
and Section
A.4

give an f-lifting
f_{F,*}:\DFb(\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)\longrightarrow\DFb(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)
of

f_{*}:\Db(\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)\longrightarrow\Db(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},E)
. Taking the limit of these f-liftings, we get an f-lifting

\DFbh(X,E)\longrightarrow\DFbh(Y,E)
of
f_{*}:\Dbh(X,E)\longrightarrow\Dbh(Y,E)
, and it is easy to check that it sends

\DFbm(X,E)
to
\DFbm(Y,E)
.

We check condition (b). Let
\mathcal{I}_{m}(f)
be the full subcategory of
f_{*}
-acyclic objects in
\Perv_{m}(X)
.
Let
\mathcal{U}=(a_{i}:U_{i}\longrightarrow X)_{i\in I}
be a finite covering of
X
by open affine subschemes. We denote by

\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U})
the full subcategory of
\Perv_{m}(X)
whose objects are mixed perverse sheaves that are

(f\circ a_{i})_{*}a_{i}^{*}
-acyclic for every
i\in I
; equivalently,
\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U})
is the full subcategory of

\bigoplus_{i\in I}(f\circ a_{i})_{*}a_{i}^{*}
-acyclic objects. As all the functors
a_{i*}a_{i}^{*}
are t-exact,
we have
\mathcal{I}_{m}(f)\subset\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U})
. By Remark
A.3.3
(2) (applied to
the triangulated categories
\Kb(\Perv_{m}(X))\supset\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U}))\supset\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f))
), to check condition (b) of
Proposition
A.3.2
, it suffices to prove the following two statements:

(1)

For every
A^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
, there exists a quasi-isomorphism
B^{\bullet}\longrightarrow A^{\bullet}
with

B^{\bullet}
in
\Cb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U}))
.

(2)

For every
B^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U}))
, there exists a quasi-isomorphism
B^{\bullet}\longrightarrow C^{\bullet}
with

C^{\bullet}
in
\Cb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f))
.

We prove (1).
Let
A^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
, and let
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
A^{r}=0
for
r\not\in[-N,N]
.
We choose a finite family
(b_{j}:V_{j}\longrightarrow X)_{j\in J}
of open affine subschemes of
X
as in Theorem
7.1.2
,
for the fixed
f
, the family
(U_{i_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{i_{p}})_{p\in\mathbb{N},\ i_{0},\ldots,i_{p}\in I}
of open affine subschemes of
X
and the perverse sheaf

K=\bigoplus_{-N\leq r\leq N}A^{r}
. Let
A_{0}^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
be the complex obtained by applying the exact functor
\bigoplus_{j\in J}b_{j!}b_{j}^{*}
to

A^{\bullet}
. Then
A_{0}^{\bullet}
is a complex of objects of
\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U})
such that
A_{0}^{r}=0
for
r\not\in[-N,N]
, and we have a morphism of complexes
A_{0}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow A^{\bullet}

that is surjective in each degree. By iterating this procedure, we get an exact sequence
\ldots\longrightarrow A_{2}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow A_{1}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow A_{0}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow A^{\bullet}\longrightarrow 0
in

\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
such that each
A_{i}^{\bullet}
is a complex of objects of
\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U})
concentrated in degrees
[-N,N]
. Moreover, by Lemma
A.3.1
(v) (whose
hypothesis is satisfied thanks to 4.2.3 and 4.2.4 of
[
BBD82
]
), we may without losing these properties assume that
A_{i}^{\bullet}=0
for
i
big enough.
We can take for
B^{\bullet}
the total complex of the double complex
A_{\bullet}^{\bullet}
.

We prove (2). For every

I^{\prime}\subset I
, we denote by
a_{I^{\prime}}:U_{I^{\prime}}:=\bigcap_{i\in I^{\prime}}U_{i}\longrightarrow X
the inclusion; if
I^{\prime}=\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}\}
, we also write

U_{I^{\prime}}=U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}
and
a_{I^{\prime}}=a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}
.
As
X
is separated, all the finite intersections of
U_{i}
’s are affine.
We first define the functor
\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}:\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
sending a
mixed perverse sheaf to its Čech resolution. If
K\in\Ob\Perv_{m}(X)
, we set

\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{r}(K)=\bigoplus_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}\in I}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}*}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}^{*}K.

The differential
d^{r}:\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{r}(\mathcal{F})\longrightarrow\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{r+1}(\mathcal{F})
is an alternating sum of adjunction morphisms.
More precisely, for
i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}\in I
, the restriction to
a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}*}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}^{*}K
of
d^{r}

is the sum over
i\in I
and
0\leq s\leq r+1
of
(-1)^{s}
times the adjunction morphism
a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}*}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}^{*}K\longrightarrow a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{s-1},i,i_{s},\ldots,i_{r}*}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{s-1},i,i_{s},\ldots,,i_{r}}^{*}K

coming from the inclusion of
U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r},i}
into
U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}
.
This defines an exact functor
\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}:\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Cpl(\Perv_{m}(X))
. We also have a natural morphism

K\longrightarrow\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}(K)
, for every
K\in\Ob\Perv_{m}(K)
, given by the sum of the adjunction morphisms.

We need a variant that takes its values in the category of bounded complexes,
called the
alternating Čech complex
(see
[
Sta24
,
Section 01FG
]
).
Let
K\in\Ob\Perv_{m}(X)
.
For every
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we have an action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{r+1}
, seen as the group of permutations of
\{0,1,\ldots,r\}
,
on the perverse sheaf
\Cech^{r}_{\mathcal{U}}(K)
: if
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{r+1}
and
i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}\in I
,
then this action sends the component
a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}*}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}^{*}K
to
a_{i_{\sigma^{-1}(0)},\ldots,i_{\sigma^{-1}(r)}*}a_{i_{\sigma^{-1}(0)},\ldots,i_{\sigma^{-1}(r)}}^{*}K=a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}*}a_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}}^{*}K
,
and it acts by
(-1)^{\sigma}\mathrm{id}
. We denote by
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{r}}(K)\subset\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{r}(K)
the invariants of this action.
This defines a subcomplex
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
of
\Cech^{\bullet}(K)
. Also, as a perverse sheaf on
X
,

\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{r}}(K)
is isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{I^{\prime}\subset I,\ |I^{\prime}|=r+1}a_{I^{\prime}*}a_{I^{\prime}}^{*}K
; in particular, we have
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{1}}(K)=\Cech^{1}_{\mathcal{U}}(K)
, so the morphism

K\longrightarrow\Cech^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{U}}(K)
factors through a morphism
K\longrightarrow\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{1}}(K)
.
Another consequence of this observation is that the subfunctor
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}
of
\Cech_{\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}
is
still exact, and that it takes its values in the full subcategory
\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
of
\Cpl(\Perv_{m}(X))
.

Lemma 7.1.3
.

Let
K\in\Ob\Perv_{m}(X)
.

(i)

The morphism of complexes
K\longrightarrow\Cech^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{U}}(K)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(ii)

The inclusion
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)\longrightarrow\Cech^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{U}}(K)
is a homotopy equivalence.

(iii)

The morphism of complexes
K\longrightarrow\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Point (iii) follows from (i) and (ii), and (ii) is proved as
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 01FM
]
.
To prove (i), we can restrict the complexes to every open subset of an open covering of
X
, for example the covering
\mathcal{U}
. So we may assume that

X
is equal to one of the
U_{i}
. In that case the complex
K\longrightarrow\Cech^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{U}}(K)
(with
K
in degree
-1
) is homotopy equivalent to
0
, see
for example the proof of
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 0G6S
]
.
∎

Now let
B^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U}))
. The double complex
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(B^{\bullet})
is bounded, and we denote its total complex by
C^{\bullet}
. By the
definition of
\mathcal{I}_{m}(f,\mathcal{U})
, this is a complex of
f_{*}
-acyclic objects, and by Lemma
7.1.3
(iii), the canonical morphism
B^{\bullet}\longrightarrow C^{\bullet}
is a
quasi-isomorphism. This gives the conclusion of (2).

Proposition
A.3.2
now says that, if
T:\Db\Perv_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{m}(Y)
is the composition of a quasi-inverse of the equivalence

\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f))/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f))
(where, for every additive subcategory
\mathcal{C}
of
\Perv(X)
, we denote by
\Nb(\mathcal{C})
the full category of exact complexes in

\Kb(\mathcal{C})
) and of the functor
\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f))/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{m}(f))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Db\Perv(Y)
, then
T
is well-defined and we have
\mathrm{real}\circ T\simeq f_{*}\circ\mathrm{real}
.

Note also that the proofs of statements (1) and (2), and the construction and properties of the Čech complex and alternating Čech complex, would work just as well
for the categories
\Perv_{mf}
, because we know that, for
g
a morphism of
k
-schemes, the functors
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{k}g_{*}
preserve the subcategories

\Perv_{mf}
(Corollary
6.3.1
). Denote by
\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f)
the full subcategory of
f_{*}
-acyclic objects in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
. We get in particular
that the obvious functor
\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f))/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f))\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
is an equivalence of categories and that
\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})
sends
objects of
\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(X))
to exact complexes, so we can make the following definition.

Definition 7.1.4
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
. We define the functor

H_{mf,*}(f):\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db_{mf}\Perv(Y)

to be the composition

\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f))/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y),

where the first functor is right adjoint to the obvious functor
\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f))/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f))\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

6
6

6

If a functor
F
is an equivalence of categories, then it admits a right adjoint, and any right adjoint is a quasi-inverse. We use
a right adjoint instead of an arbitrary quasi-inverse, because a right adjoint is unique up to unique isomorphism.

It remains to show that Definition
7.1.4

does give a
2
-functor from
\mathbf{Sch}/k
to
\mathfrak{TR}
, that is, to construct connection morphisms.
So suppose that we have two morphisms of
k
-schemes
f:X\longrightarrow Y
and
g:Y\longrightarrow Z
. We want
to lift the connection isomorphism
g_{*}\circ f_{*}\simeq(g\circ f)_{*}:\Dbm(X)\longrightarrow\Dbm(Z)
to a
natural isomorphism
H_{mf,*}(g)\circ H_{mf,*}(f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{mf,*}(g\circ f)
.
Let us write
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f)
,
\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{mf}(g\circ f)
,
\mathcal{I}_{3}=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f\times(g\circ f))
and
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}_{mf}(g)
.
We consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{2})/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\Kb({}^{0}(g\circ f)_{*})}
\scriptstyle{(*)}
\textstyle{\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{3})/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{32}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{31}}
\scriptstyle{\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})}
\textstyle{\Kb(\mathcal{I}_{1})/\Nb(\mathcal{I}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})}
\textstyle{\Kb(\mathcal{J})/\Nb(\mathcal{J})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\Kb({}^{0}g_{*})}
\textstyle{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}
\textstyle{\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)}
\textstyle{\Db\Perv_{mf}(Z)}

All the vertical maps in it are equivalences, and the diagram commutes, except for triangle (*) that only commutes up to a natural isomorphism
coming from the connection isomorphism
(g\circ f)_{*}\simeq g_{*}\circ f_{*}
.

We also set
\Phi_{3}=\Phi_{1}\circ\Phi_{31}=\Phi_{2}\circ\Phi_{32}
. For every arrow
\Phi_{?}
, we denote by
\Psi_{?}
a right adjoint of
\Phi_{?}
.
Then we have natural transformations

\displaystyle H_{mf,*}(g\circ f)=\Kb({}^{0}(g\circ f)_{*})\circ\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\Kb({}^{0}(g\circ f)_{*})\circ\Phi_{32}\circ\Psi_{32}\circ\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle\simeq\Kb({}^{0}(g\circ f)_{*})\circ\Phi_{32}\circ\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\simeq}}\Kb({}^{0}g_{*})\circ\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})\circ\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Kb({}^{0}g_{*})\circ\Psi\circ\Phi\circ\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})\circ\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle=H_{mf,*}(g)\circ\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})\circ\Phi_{31}\circ\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle\simeq H_{mf,*}(g)\circ\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})\circ\Phi_{31}\circ\Psi_{31}\circ\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H_{mf,*}(g)\circ\Kb({}^{0}f_{*})\circ\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle=H_{mf,*}(g)\circ H_{mf,*}(f).

Moreover, all these natural transformations are isomorphisms: the ones marked
\leftarrow
and
\rightarrow
are units or counits of
adjunctions between equivalences of categories, isomorphism (*) comes from the connection isomorphism
(g\circ f)_{*}\simeq g_{*}\circ f_{*}
, and
the two remaining isomorphisms come from the uniqueness of right adjoints.
This gives the desired connection isomorphism, and we check the cocycle condition in a similar way.
This finishes the proof of Proposition
7.1.1
.

Remark 7.1.5
.

Suppose that
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is an affine morphism of
k
-schemes. By Theorem
7.1.2
(and Corollary
6.3.1
)),
the category
\mathcal{I}_{mf}(f_{*})
of
f_{*}
-acyclic objects in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
is cogenerating. So, by Remark
A.3.3
(1), the functor

{}^{0}f_{*}:\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(Y)
has a left derived functor, and the functor

H_{mf,*}(f)
is the restriction of this derived functor to
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
. In particular, if
f
is quasi-finite and affine,
then
f_{*}:\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(Y)
is exact, and
H_{mf,*}(f)
is its obvious extension to the bounded derived categories.

From now on, for
f:X\longrightarrow Y
a morphism of
k
-schemes,
we will also denote the functor
H_{mf,*}(f)
by
f_{*}
if there is no risk of confusion.

Proposition 7.1.6
.

The functor
\boxtimes
from Proposition
4.2

induces a natural isomorphism between the
2
-functors
H_{mf,*}\times H_{mf,*}:\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\times\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
and
\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\times\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H_{mf,*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{TR}
(where the first arrow sends
(X,Y)
to
X\times Y
).

In other words, if
f_{1}:X_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{1}
and
f_{2}:X_{2}\longrightarrow Y_{2}
are morphisms
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
and
K_{1}\in D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X_{1})
,
K_{2}\in D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X_{2})
, then
we have an isomorphism

(f_{1}\times f_{2})_{*}(K_{1}\boxtimes K_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(f_{1*}K_{1})\boxtimes(f_{2*}K_{2})

functorial in
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
and compatible with the composition of
arrows in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
.

Proof.

On the categories
\Dbc
, we have canonical isomorphisms
(see SGA 5 III 1.6). These induce similar isomorphisms in the categories
\Dbh
and

\Dbm
.

By the construction of
f_{*}
(see Definition
7.1.4
),
we only need to show the statement of the proposition for
the functors
{}^{0}f_{*}
between the categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.
But then it is an immediate consequence of the similar result for the
categories
\Perv(X)
, which follows from the result recalled at the beginning of the proof and from
the t-exactness of the external tensor
product (see Proposition
2.2.2
).
∎

Proposition 7.1.7
.

Let
j:U\longrightarrow X
be an open embedding.
Denote by
j^{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U)
the derived functor
of the exact functor
j^{*}:\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(U)
.

Then this functor
j^{*}
is left adjoint to the functor

j_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)
, and
the counit map

j^{*}j_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be a finite open affine cover of
U
. For every

J\subset I
, we denote by
a_{J}:\bigcap_{i\in J}U_{i}\longrightarrow U
the inclusion.
As
U
is separated, all the finite intersections of
U_{i}
’s are affine,
so the morphisms
a_{J}
and
j\circ a_{J}
are affine for every
J\subset I
.
If
K\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(U)
, we denote by
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
the alternating Čech complex
of
K
associated to the covering
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
(defined on page
7.1
), so that

\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{r}}(K)=\bigoplus_{|J|=r+1}a_{J*}a_{J}^{*}K
.
The canonical morphism

K\longrightarrow\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\bullet}^{\mathcal{U}}}(K)
is a quasi-isomorphism (Lemma
7.1.3
(iii)), and all the
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{r}}(K)
are

j_{*}
-acyclic (indeed, as
j\circ a_{J}
is affine for every
J\subset I
, the complex

j_{*}(a_{J*}a_{J}^{*}K)
is perverse, and so
j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{r}}(K)
is perverse).

As the alternating Čech complex is a functor
\Perv_{mf}(U)\longrightarrow\Cb(\Perv_{mf}(U))
,
we have by Definition
7.1.4
an isomorphism of functors between
j_{*}
and the functor
sending
K
to the the total complex of the double complex

j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
. So
j^{*}j_{*}
is isomorphic to the functor sending
K

to the total complex of
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
. Let

\alpha:\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow j^{*}j_{*}
the map corresponding to the natural transformation

K\longrightarrow\Tot\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
. This is an isomorphism of endofunctors
of
\Db\Perv_{mf}(U)
by Lemma
7.1.3
(iii)), and we set

\varepsilon=\alpha^{-1}
; this is the counit of the adjunction.

Now we construct the unit
\eta:\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}
.
As
j^{*}
is exact, the functor
j^{*}j_{*}
is isomorphic to the functor sending

L\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
to the total complex of the double complex
j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)
.
If
L
is a finite complex of objects of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
, then
j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{0}}(j^{*}L)=\bigoplus_{i\in I}(j\circ a_{i})_{*}(j\circ a_{i})^{*}L
;
taking the sum of the unit morphisms of the adjunctions
((j\circ a_{i})^{*},(j\circ a_{i})_{*})
,
we get a morphism
L\longrightarrow j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{0}}(j^{*}L)
in
\Cb(\Perv_{m}(X))
, hence in its full subcategory
\Cb(\Perv_{mf}(X))
.
Also, it is easy to see that the composition
L\longrightarrow j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{0}}(j^{*}L)\longrightarrow j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{1}}(j^{*}L)
is
0
in

\Cb(\Perv_{mf}(X))
. So we get a morphism from
L
to the total complex of the double complex
j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)
, which
induces
\eta:\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}
.

To finish the proof, it suffices to
show that, for
K\in\Ob D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(U)
and
L\in\Ob D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)
,
the composition

j_{*}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta j_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{*}j^{*}j_{*}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{*}\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{*}K

is the identity of
j_{*}K
and the composition

j^{*}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{*}\eta}}{{\longrightarrow}}j^{*}j_{*}j^{*}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon j^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}j^{*}L

is the identity of
j^{*}L
.

Let
K\in\Cb(\Perv_{mf}(U))
. We denote by
s
the canonical morphism from
K
to the total complex of
\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
.
Using the canonical isomorphism
j^{*}\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)=\Tot\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)
, we
get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{j_{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta j_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}j_{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{j_{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(s)}
\textstyle{\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(\Tot\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K))}
\textstyle{\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(s)}

This gives the first statement.

Let
L\in\Cb(\Perv_{mf}(X))
. We denote by
s
the canonical morphism
j^{*}L\longrightarrow\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)
, and by

s^{\prime}
the morphism from
L
to
\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)
from the construction of
\eta
. We have a canonical isomorphism

j^{*}\Tot j_{*}\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)=\Tot\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)
, and this identifies
j^{*}s^{\prime}
and
s
. So we get a commutative
diagram

\textstyle{j^{*}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{j^{*}j_{*}j^{*}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{j^{*}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\Tot\mathrm{\check{C}_{alt,\mathcal{U}}^{\bullet}}(j^{*}L)}

This gives the second statement.
∎

7.2
Inverse images

In this section, we construct the inverse images functors as the left
adjoints of the direct image functors of Proposition
7.1.1
.

First we treat a particular case. For every smooth equidimensional

k
-scheme
X
, we denote by
1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
the constant sheaf on
X
,
seen as an object of
\Perv_{mf}(X)[-\dim X]
.

Proposition 7.2.1
.

Let
X,Y\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, and suppose that
X
is smooth
equidimensional.
Let
p:X\times Y\longrightarrow Y
be the second projection.

Then the functor
p_{*}:D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X\times Y)\longrightarrow D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(Y)

admits a left adjoint
p^{*}
, which is given
by
K\longmapsto 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes K
.

In particular, we get a natural isomorphism
\theta_{p}:p^{*}\circ R_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R_{X\times Y}\circ p^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
p^{*}
be as in the statement.
It suffices to construct
natural morphisms
\eta:\mathrm{id}\longrightarrow p_{*}p^{*}

and
\varepsilon:p^{*}p_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}
whose images by
R
are the unit and counit of
the adjonction in the categories
D^{b}\Perv_{m}
, and such that

p_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta p_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}p_{*}p^{*}p_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{*}\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}p_{*}
is the identity.
(As
R_{X\times Y}
is conservative, we’ll automatically get the fact
that
p^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon p^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}p^{*}p_{*}p^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{*}\eta}}{{\longrightarrow}}p^{*}
is an
isomorphism.)

Let
a_{X}:X\longrightarrow\Spec k
be the structural map.
Note that, as
a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\in\Dpos\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})&=&\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},\mathrm{H}^{0}a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\\
&=&\Hom_{\Perv(\Spec k)}(E,\mathrm{H}^{0}a_{X*}\underline{E}_{X}).\end{array}

So the canonical morphism

E\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}a_{X*}\underline{E}_{X}
(coming from the unit of the
adjunction
(a_{X}^{*},a_{X*})
gives a morphism
u_{X}:1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
in
\Db\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)
.

If
K\in\Ob(\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y))
, then we have a morphism

K=1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\boxtimes K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{X}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\boxtimes K\simeq p_{*}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes K)=p_{*}p^{*}K,

where the third arrow is the isomorphism of Proposition

7.1.6
. This morphism is an isomorphism because
its image by
R_{Y}
is an isomorphism, and we denote it
by
\eta
.

Now we want to construct
\varepsilon
. Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X\times X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{q_{1}}
\textstyle{X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}

where
q_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{X}\times p
,
q_{2}=a_{X}\times\mathrm{id}_{X\times Y}
and
i
is the
product of the diagonal embedding of
X
and of
\mathrm{id}_{Y}
. Note that

q_{1}i=q_{2}i=p
.
Using Proposition
7.1.6
, we get an isomorphism

p^{*}p_{*}K=1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes(p_{*}K)\simeq q_{1*}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes K)=q_{1*}q_{2}^{*}K.

As
i
is a closed immersion, we know (by Corollary
6.4.2
) that
the functor
i_{*}
has a left adjoint
i^{*}
. This and the functoriality of

H_{mf,*}
gives a morphism

q_{1*}q_{2}^{*}K\longrightarrow q_{1*}i_{*}i^{*}q_{2}^{*}K\simeq q_{2*}i_{*}i^{*}q_{2}^{*}K.

Note also that using
the unit of
(i^{*},i_{*})
and the analogue of the natural transformation

\eta
for

q_{2}
instead of
p
, we get a morphism

K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}q_{2*}q_{2}^{*}K\longrightarrow q_{2*}i_{*}i^{*}q_{2}^{*}K,

which is an isomorphism because its image by
R_{X\times Y}
is an isomorphism.
Putting all these together gives

\varepsilon:p^{*}p_{*}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}q_{1*}q_{2}^{*}K\longrightarrow q_{2*}i_{*}i^{*}q_{2}^{*}K\simeq K.

It is clear from the construction
that the images of
\eta
and
\varepsilon
by
R
are the unit
and the counit of the adjonction
(p^{*},p_{*})
in
\Dbm
.
So we just need to show that
p_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta p_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}p_{*}p^{*}p_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{*}\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}p_{*}
is the identity.
This follows from the fact that
we get this composition by following the
outside of the commutative diagram below in the clockwise direction (where the
two arrows marked “
\mathrm{adj}
” come from the unit of the adjunction

(i^{*},i_{*})
) :

\textstyle{p_{*}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes(p_{*}K))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{p_{*}p^{*}p_{*}K}
\textstyle{1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\boxtimes(p_{*}K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{X}}
\textstyle{(a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\boxtimes(p_{*}K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{p_{*}q_{1*}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{p_{*}q_{1*}q_{2}^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{adj}}
\textstyle{p_{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{p_{*}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\boxtimes K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{X}}
\textstyle{p_{*}q_{1*}i_{*}i^{*}q_{2}^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{p_{*}((a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\boxtimes K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{p_{*}q_{2*}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p_{*}q_{2*}q_{2}^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{adj}}
\textstyle{p_{*}q_{2*}i_{*}i^{*}q_{2}^{*}K}

∎

Having at our disposal
the constant sheaf on
X
was very important when constructing
the inverse image of the second projection
X\times Y\longrightarrow Y
.
Now, in order to generalize this construction to the case when
X

is not necessarily smooth,
we want to construct (and characterize) the analogue
in
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
of the constant sheaf
\underline{E}_{X}
.
Note that this is not totally obvious
in this context because, if
X
is not smooth,
then the constant sheaf is not perverse (or shifted perverse) in general.

For every
k
-scheme
X
, we denote by
a_{X}:X\longrightarrow\Spec k
the structural
morphism. We also denote by
1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}
the constant sheaf with
value
E
on
\Spec k
, seen as an object of
\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)
.

Corollary 7.2.2
.

For every
k
-scheme
X
, the functor
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sets}
(where
\mathrm{Sets}
is the category of sets),

K\longmapsto\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},a_{X*}K)
, is representable.

Moreover, if
(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X},u_{X}:1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})
represents this functor, then there is an isomorphism

R_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\simeq\underline{E}_{X}
that makes the following
diagram commute :

\textstyle{R_{\Spec k}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{\Spec k}(u_{X})}
\textstyle{\underline{E}_{\Spec k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{adj}}
\textstyle{a_{X*}\underline{E}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{R_{\Spec k}(a_{X*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{a_{X}}}
\textstyle{a_{X*}R_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})}

where the arrow marked “
\mathrm{adj}
” is the unit of the adjunction
(a_{X}^{*},a_{X*})
.

Note that the couple
(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X},u_{X})
is unique up
to unique isomorphism if it exists.

Proof.

First note that, thanks to Corollary

6.4.2
and Proposition

7.1.7
, if
h:Z\longrightarrow X
is an open embedding or
a closed embedding and
the result is true for
X
, then it is also true for
Z
, and moreover we
have a canonical isomorphism
1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{Z}\simeq h^{*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
.
Moreover, if
X
is smooth, then the result follows immediately
from Proposition
7.2.1
. In particular,
we get the result for
X
affine, because in that case
X
is a closed
subscheme of some
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.

For a general
k
-scheme
X
, we chose a finite open cover
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
such that the result is known for every
U_{i}
. (For example, we can
take a finite affine open cover.)
We want to show that this implies the result for

X
. We reduce to the case
n=2
by an easy induction on
n
. Let
j_{1}:U_{1}\longrightarrow X
,
j_{2}:U_{2}\longrightarrow X
and
j_{12}:U_{1}\cap U_{2}\longrightarrow X
be the inclusions. By the
uniqueness statement of the corollary,
we have canonical isomorphisms
1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{i}|U_{1}\cap U_{2}}\simeq 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
for
i=1,2
that identify
u_{U_{i}}
and

u_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
,
so, using Proposition

7.1.7
, we get morphisms
v_{i}:j_{i*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{i}}\longrightarrow j_{12,*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
,
i=1,2
. Complete
v:=v_{1}\oplus(-v_{2})
into
an exact triangle

(*)\hskip 20.00003ptK\longrightarrow j_{1*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\oplus j_{2*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{12*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}

Applying
a_{X*}
, we get a triangle

(**)\hskip 20.00003pta_{X*}K\longrightarrow a_{U_{1},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\oplus a_{U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{X*}v}}{{\longrightarrow}}a_{U_{1}\cap U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}

Consider the morphism
u_{U_{1}}\oplus u_{U_{2}}:1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{U_{1},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\oplus a_{U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}
. Composing it by
a_{X*}v
gives

0
, by definition of
v
, so it comes from a map
u_{X}:1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{X*}K
. Also, as
a_{U_{1}\cap U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
is concentrated in degree

\geq 0
, we have
\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},a_{U_{1}\cap U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}[-1])=0
, and so the map
u_{X}
is uniquely determined.

Now we show that
(K,u_{X})
represents the functor of the statement.
For every
L\in\Ob(D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X))
, the map
u_{X}:1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{X*}K

induces a morphism

\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,L)\rightarrow\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(a_{X*}K,a_{X*}L)\rightarrow\Hom_{D^{b}\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},a_{X*}L),

and we must
show that this is an isomorphism. Suppose that we can prove this
if one of the adjunction maps

L\longrightarrow j_{1*}j_{1}^{*}L
,
L\longrightarrow j_{2*}j_{2}^{*}L
or
L\longrightarrow j_{12*}j_{12}^{*}L
is an
isomorphism, then we are done. Indeed,
for a general
L
, we have an exact triangle

L\longrightarrow j_{1*}j_{1}^{*}L\oplus j_{2*}j_{2}^{*}L\longrightarrow j_{12*}j_{12}^{*}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
, and we use
the five lemma.

Suppose that the adjunction map
L\longrightarrow j_{1*}j_{1}^{*}L
is an isomorphism.
Applying
j_{1}^{*}
to the triangle
(*)
and noting that
j_{1}^{*}j_{2*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}\longrightarrow j_{1}^{*}j_{12*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
is an isomorphism, we get an isomorphism

j_{1}^{*}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}
.
We denote by
c
the
base change morphism
a_{X*}\longrightarrow a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}
. Applying
c
to the entries of the triangle

(**)
, we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a_{U_{1},*}j_{1}^{*}j_{1*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\oplus a_{U_{1},*}j_{1}^{*}j_{2*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}j_{12*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{a_{X*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a_{U_{1},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\oplus a_{U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a_{U_{1}\cap U_{2},*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}

The morphism
a_{U_{1},*}j_{1}^{*}j_{2*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}\longrightarrow a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}j_{12*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
in the first row of this diagram is an isomorphism,
so we get an isomorphism

a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}K\longrightarrow a_{U_{1},*}j_{1}^{*}j_{1*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\simeq a_{U_{1}*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}
(which is
just the image by
a_{U_{1}*}
of the isomorphism
j_{1}^{*}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}
of the beginning of
this paragraph.
By this isomorphism, the map

c_{K}\circ u_{X}:1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}K
corresponds to the composition
of

(c_{j_{1*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}}\oplus c_{j_{2*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}})\circ(u_{U_{1}}\oplus u_{U_{2}}):1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\longrightarrow a_{U_{1},*}j_{1}^{*}j_{1*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}\oplus a_{U_{1},*}j_{1}^{*}j_{2*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{2}}

and of the first projection. In other words, we get a commutative diagram :

\textstyle{1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{X}}
\scriptstyle{u_{U_{1}}}
\textstyle{a_{X*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{K}}
\textstyle{a_{U_{1}*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}}}
\textstyle{a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

Consider the following diagram (where all the
\Hom
groups are taken in the appropriate

\Db\Perv_{mf}
category) :

\textstyle{\Hom(K,j_{1*}j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{X*}}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}^{*}}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom(a_{X*}K,a_{X*}j_{1*}j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\circ u_{X}}
\textstyle{\Hom(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(j_{1}^{*},j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{U_{1}*}}
\textstyle{\Hom(a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}K,a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\circ c_{K}}
\textstyle{\Hom(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}},j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{U_{1}*}}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom(a_{U_{1}*}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{U_{1}},a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{(-)\circ u_{U_{1}}}
\textstyle{\Hom(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k},a_{U_{1}*}j_{1}^{*}L)}

We have just seen that the right rectangle of this diagram is commutative. It is also easy to see
that the two squares on the left are commutative, so the whole diagram commutes. As
the composition of the two bottom horizontal
arrows is an isomorphism by assumption, the composition of the
two top horizontal arrows is also an isomorphism, which is what we wanted to prove.

The case where
L\longrightarrow j_{2*}j_{2}^{*}L
(resp.
L\longrightarrow j_{12*}j_{12}^{*}L
) is an
isomorphism is similar. This finishes the proof of the first statement of the corollary.
The second statement of the corollary follows easily from the explicit definition of
u_{X}
.
∎

Now that we have the object
1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
, the proof of the following
corollary is exactly the same as the proof of Proposition

7.2.1
.

Corollary 7.2.3
.

Let
X,Y\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, and let

p:X\times Y\longrightarrow Y
be the second projection.

Then the functor

p_{*}:\Db\Perv_{mf}(X\times Y)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)

admits a left adjoint
p^{*}
, which is given
by
K\longmapsto 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}\boxtimes K
.

Corollary 7.2.4
.

The
2
-functor
H_{mf,*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
of Proposition

7.1.1
admits a global left adjoint in the sense
of Definition 1.1.18 of
[
Ayo07
]
.

In particular, we get a uniquely determined

2
-functor
H_{mf}^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
such, for every morphism

f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, the functor

H_{mf}^{*}(f):\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
is a left adjoint of

H_{mf,*}(f):\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)
.

Moreover, for every morphism of
k
-schemes
f:X\longrightarrow Y
, we have an
invertible natural transformation
\theta_{f}:H_{m}^{*}(f)\circ R_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R_{X}\circ H_{mf}^{*}(f)
, and this is compatible with the composition of morphisms
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
.

Proof.

By Proposition 1.1.17 of
[
Ayo07
]
, to show the first statement,
it suffices to show
that, for every
f:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
, the functor

H_{mf,*}(f):\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)
admits a left adjoint.
We factor
f
as
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}X\times Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y
,
where
i=\mathrm{id}_{X}\times f
and
p
is the second projection.
The first map is a closed embedding, so it admits a left adjoint by
Corollary
6.4.2
, and the second map admits a left adjoint by
Corollary
7.2.3
.
The natural transformation
\theta_{i}
and
\theta_{p}
are also constructed in
these corollaries, and we take
\theta_{f}
equal to :

R_{X}\circ f^{*}=R_{X}\circ i^{*}p^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}i^{*}\circ R_{X\times Y}\circ p^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}i^{*}p^{*}\circ R_{Y}\simeq f^{*}\circ R_{Y}.

By a slight abuse of notation, we will write that
\theta_{f}=\theta_{p}\circ\theta_{i}
.

Suppose that we are given a second morphism
g:Y\longrightarrow Z
, and that we are
trying to prove the compatibility between
\theta_{f}
,
\theta_{g}
and

\theta_{gf}
. Consider the commutative diagram :

\textstyle{X\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X\times Y\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y\times Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z}

where
i^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}\times g
,
i^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=(x,y,g(y))
,
i^{\prime\prime\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{X}\times(gf)
and

p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime\prime},q
are the obvious projections.
Then
\theta_{g}=\theta_{p^{\prime}}\circ\theta_{i^{\prime}}
and
\theta_{gf}=\theta_{p^{\prime\prime\prime}}\circ\theta_{i^{\prime\prime\prime}}
. So it suffices to prove that :

(a)

\theta_{q}\circ\theta_{i^{\prime\prime}i}=\theta_{i^{\prime\prime\prime}}
;

(b)

\theta_{i^{\prime\prime}i}=\theta_{i^{\prime\prime}}\theta_{i}
;

(c)

\theta_{p^{\prime\prime}}\circ\theta_{q}=\theta_{p^{\prime}}\circ\theta_{p^{\prime\prime}}
;

(d)

\theta_{p^{\prime\prime}}\circ\theta_{i^{\prime\prime}}=\theta_{i^{\prime}}\circ\theta_{p}
.

Point (b) follows from Corollary
6.4.2
and point (c) from the
explicit formula for the inverse image of a projection in
Corollary
7.2.3
.
The other two compatibilities can easily be proved directly.
∎

Finally, we have :

Proposition 7.2.5
.

The functor
\boxtimes
of Proposition
4.2

induces a natural isomorphism between the
2
-functors
H_{mf}^{*}\times H_{mf}^{*}:\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\times\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
and
\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\times\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Sch}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H_{mf}^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{TR}
, where the first arrow sends
(X,Y)
to
X\times Y
.

In other words, if
f_{1}:X_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{1}
and
f_{2}:X_{2}\longrightarrow Y_{2}
are morphisms
in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
and
L_{1}\in\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y_{1})
,
L_{2}\in\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y_{2})
, then
we have an isomorphism

(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(L_{1}\boxtimes L_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(f_{1}^{*}L_{1})\boxtimes(f_{2}^{*}L_{2})

functorial in
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
and compatible with the composition of
arrows in
\mathbf{Sch}/k
.

Proof.

By the construction of the functors
f^{*}
above,
we only need to show the statement when
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both closed
