immersions, or when they are both projections. If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both
projections, the result is obvious. If they are both closed
immersions, the result follows from the construction in the proof of
Corollary
6.4.2
and from Proposition
7.1.6
.
∎

7.3
Poincaré-Verdier duality

Just as in sections
7.1
and
7.2
,
we can prove the
following result.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

There exists a
2
-functor
H_{mf,!}:\mathbf{Sch}/k\longrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

with
H_{mf,!}(X)=\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
and
a natural transformation
R:H_{mf,!}\longrightarrow H_{m,!}
(with the notation
of Example
3.2.3
) such that :

(a)

for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
, the functor
R_{X}:\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbm(X)
is the functor of Theorem
3.2.4
;

(b)

for every morphism

f:X\longrightarrow Y
, the natural transformation

\rho_{f}:R_{Y}\circ H_{mf,!}(f)\longrightarrow H_{m,!}(f)\circ R_{X}
is an isomorphism.

This functor satisfies the same compatibility with
\boxtimes
as in
Proposition
7.1.6
, and it admits a global
right adjoint
H_{mf}^{!}
.

Moreover, by
Proposition
4.2
, we have an exact contravariant endofunctor

D_{X}
of
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
together with an isomorphism
D_{X}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{id}
,
for every
X\in\Ob(\mathbf{Sch}/k)
.

Proposition 7.3.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a morphism of
k
-schemes.

(i)

We have a natural isomorphism
\alpha_{f}:f_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{Y}\circ f_{!}\circ D_{X}

such that, if
g:Y\longrightarrow Z
is another morphism of
k
-schemes, then the
isomorphism
\alpha_{gf}:(gf)_{*}\simeq D_{Z}(gf)_{!}D_{X}
is equal to the isomorphism

(gf)_{*}\simeq g_{*}f_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{g}\alpha_{f}}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{Z}g_{!}D_{Y}D_{Y}f_{!}D_{X}\simeq D_{Z}g_{!}f_{!}D_{X}\simeq D_{Z}(gf)_{!}D_{X}

where the first and fourth arrows are given by the composition isomorphisms
of the
2
-functors
H_{mf,*}
and
H_{mf,!}
, and the third arrow is given
by the isomorphism
D_{Y}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{id}
.

(ii)

We have a natural isomorphism
\beta_{f}:f^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{X}\circ f^{!}\circ D_{Y}
, where
f^{!}=H_{mf}^{!}(f)
,
such that, if
g:Y\longrightarrow Z
is another morphism of
k
-schemes, then the
isomorphism
\beta_{gf}(gf)^{*}\simeq D_{X}(gf)^{!}D_{Z}
is equal to the isomorphism

(gf)^{*}\simeq f^{*}g^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta_{f}\beta_{g}}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{X}f^{!}D_{Y}D_{Y}g^{!}D_{Z}\simeq D_{X}f^{!}g^{!}D_{Z}\simeq D_{X}(gf)^{!}D_{Z}

where the first and fourth arrows are given by the composition isomorphisms
of the
2
-functors
H_{mf}^{*}
and
H_{mf}^{!}
, and the third arrow is given
by the isomorphism
D_{Y}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{id}
.

(iii)

If
f
is smooth and purely of relative dimension
d
, then
we have an natural isomorphism
f^{!}[-d]\simeq f^{*}[d](d)
of functors

\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

(iv)

If
f
is smooth and purely of relative dimension
d
, then
the functor
f^{*}:\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
admits a left
adjoint
f_{\sharp}
.

(v)

If
i:X\longrightarrow Y
is a closed immersion, then we have a natural
isomorphism
i_{!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{*}
.

Proof.

Point (iii) follows from the fact that both functors are t-exact and that
such an isomorphism exists in the category of functors

\Perv_{mf}(Y)\longrightarrow\Perv_{mf}(X)
(because it does for mixed perverse sheaves
and the categories
\Perv_{mf}
are full subcategories of the categories of
mixed perverse sheaves).

Point (iv) follows from point (iii) : take
f_{\sharp}=f_{!}[2d](d)
.

Point (v) is proved like point (iii) : both functors are

t
-exact, and the natural isomorphism exists when we see
i_{!}
and
i_{*}
as
functors from
\Perv_{m}(X)
to
\Perv_{m}(Y)
.

Let’s prove (i).
By the construction of
f_{*}
in section
7.1

(and point (iii) applied to inverse images by open immersions)
it suffices to prove the analogous result
for the functors
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}f_{*}
and
{}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{0}f_{!}
if
f
is affine. But then this
follows from the case of the categories
\Dbc(X)
.

Point (ii) now follows from (i) and from the uniqueness of adjoint functors.
∎

8
Tensor products and internal Homs

Definition 8.1
.

Let
X
be a
k
-scheme. We denote by
\Delta_{X}:X\longrightarrow X\times X

the diagonal embedding. We define a functor
\otimes_{X}:(\Db\Perv_{mf}(X))^{2}\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
by
K\otimes_{X}L=\Delta_{X}^{*}(K\boxtimes L)
.

Note that it follows from Proposition
7.2.5

that, for every morphism of
k
-schemes
f:X\longrightarrow Y
and
all
K,L\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(Y)
, we have a canonical isomorphism

f^{*}(K\otimes_{Y}L)\simeq(f^{*}K)\otimes_{X}(f^{*}L).

Proposition 8.2
.

The operation
\otimes_{X}
defined above makes

\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
into a symmetric monoïdal
triangulated category. Also, the object
1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
constructed
in Corollary
7.2.2
is a unit for
\otimes_{X}
,
and the functor
R_{X}:\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbm(X)

is symmetric monoïdal unitary.

Proof.

The first statement follows easily from the commutativity
and associativity of
\boxtimes
(which in turn follows from the similar
statement in
\Dbm(X)
, as
\boxtimes
is exact).
Moreover,
for every
K\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
, if
p:X\times X\longrightarrow X
is the
second projection, then :

K\otimes_{X}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}=\Delta_{X}^{*}(K\boxtimes 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})=\Delta_{X}^{*}p^{*}K\simeq(p\Delta_{X})^{*}K\simeq K

because
p\Delta_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
This proves the second statement. Finally, the fact that
R_{X}
is
monoïdal follows from the fact that it preserves
\boxtimes
, and
the last statement of Corollary
7.2.2

(i.e., the isomorphism
R_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\simeq\underline{E}_{X}
)
implies that
R_{X}
is
unitary.
∎

The main result of this section in the following :

Proposition 8.3
.

For every
k
-scheme
X
and every
K\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
,
the endofunctor
K\otimes_{X}\cdot
of
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
has a right adjoint

\Homf(K,\cdot)
,
given by
L\longmapsto D_{X}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L))
.
Moreover, for all
K,L,M\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
, we have a commutative
diagram

\textstyle{R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(L,D_{X}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{R\Hom_{\Dbm(X)}(R_{X}(K)\otimes_{X}R_{X}(L),R_{X}(M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{R\Hom_{\Dbm(X)}(R_{X}(L),D_{X}(R_{X}(K)\otimes_{X}D_{X}R_{X}(M)))}

where the horizontal arrows are the adjunction isomorphisms
(see Lemma
8.4
for the identification

D_{X}(R_{X}(K)\otimes_{X}D_{X}R_{X}(M))=\Homf_{X}(R_{X}(K),R_{X}(M))
).

In the lemmas that follow, we will denote the structural morphism

X\longrightarrow\Spec k
by
a
.
Remember that we write
K_{X}=a^{!}\underline{E}_{\Spec k}
for the dualizing
complex in
\Dbh(X)
. This is an object of
\Dbm(X)
(because

\underline{E}_{\Spec k}
clearly is a mixed complex, and
a^{!}

preserves mixed complexes).

Lemma 8.4
.

In the category
\Dbh(X)
,
we have a canonical isomorphism, functorial in
K
and
L
:

\Homf_{X}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})\simeq\Homf_{X}(K,D_{X}(L)).

Moreover, these complexes are concentrated in perverse degree
\geq 0

if
K
and
L
are perverse.

In particular, if we replace
L
by
D_{X}(L)
, we get a natural
isomorphism

\Homf_{X}(K,L)\simeq D_{X}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L)),

which explains the definition of the internal Hom given in
Proposition
8.3
.

Proof.

By Theorem 6.3(ii) of
[
Eke90
]
(see also the
remark following Definition 1.2 of
[
Hub97
]
for the extension of this to the
category
\Dbh(X)
),
we have a natural isomorphism

\Homf_{X}(K\otimes_{X}L,M)=\Homf_{X}(K,\Homf_{X}(L,M))

for all
K,L,M\in\Ob\Dbh(X)
.
Applying this to
M=K_{X}

gives the desired isomorphism.

If
K
and
L
are perverse, then the complex
K\otimes_{X}L
is
concentrated in perverse degree
\leq 0
(because it is equal
by definition to
\Delta_{X}^{*}(K\boxtimes L)
, where
\Delta_{X}:X\longrightarrow X\times X

is the diagonal morphism, and
\Delta_{X}^{*}
is right t-exact), so
its dual
\Homf_{X}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})
is concentrated in perverse
degree
\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

If
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{h}(X)
, then
the complex
a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)\in\Dbh(\Spec k)
is concentrated in
degree
\leq 0
, and so the adjunction
(a_{!},a^{!})
gives a canonical
isomorphism

\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})\simeq\Hom_{\Perv_{h}(\Spec k)}(\mathrm{H}^{0}(a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)),\underline{E}_{\Spec k})

and equalities

\Ext^{i}_{\Dbh(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})=0

for every
i<0
.

We could also have deduced the vanishing of
\Ext^{i}_{\Dbh(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})
for
i<0
from the adjunction isomorphism

\Ext^{i}_{\Dbh(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})=\Ext^{i}(K,D_{X}(L))
. (But we
won’t be able to do this in the next lemma, which is the analogous
statement in
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.)

Proof.

We have

a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)\simeq D_{\Spec k}(a_{*}D_{X}(K\otimes_{X}L))\simeq D_{\Spec k}(a_{*}\Homf_{X}(K,D_{X}(L))),

where the second isomorphism comes from Lemma
8.4
.
So it suffices to show that
a_{*}\Homf_{X}(K,D_{X}(L)))=R\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K,D_{X}(L))
is concentrated in degree
\geq 0
.
As
K
and
D_{X}(L)
are perverse, this just follows from the definition
of a t-structure.

Now, using the adjunction
(a_{!},a^{!})
and the fact that
K_{X}=a^{!}\underline{E}_{\Spec k}
, we get a canonical isomorphism

\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})=\Hom_{\Dbh(\Spec k)}(a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L),\underline{E}_{\Spec k}).

The second statement follows from this and from the fact that

a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)
is concentrated in degree
\leq 0
.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

If
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(X)
, then
the complex
a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)\in\Db\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)
is concentrated in
degree
\leq 0
, and so the adjunction
(a_{!},a^{!})
gives a canonical
isomorphism

\Hom_{\\
Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\simeq\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(\mathrm{H}^{0}(a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)),1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})

and equalities

\Ext^{i}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})=0

for every
i<0
.

Proof.

We have

R_{X}(a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L))\simeq a_{!}(R_{X}(K)\otimes_{X}R_{X}(L)),

so
R_{X}(a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L))
is concentrated in degree

\leq 0
by Lemma
8.5
. The first statement follows from
the conservativity of
R_{X}
. The second statement is proved exactly
as the second statement of Lemma
8.5
, using the
adjunction
(a_{!},a^{!})
in the categories
\Db\Perv_{mf}
.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(X)
, write
K^{\prime}=R_{X}(K)
,

L^{\prime}=R_{X}(L)
. Then
the morphism

R_{X}:\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,D_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}))\longrightarrow\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L^{\prime},K_{X})

is an isomorphism. In particular,
there exists a unique isomorphism

\alpha_{K,L}:\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,D_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,D_{X}(L))
, making the following diagram
commute

\textstyle{\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,D_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{K,L}}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,D_{X}(L))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L^{\prime},K_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\Perv_{h}(X)}(K^{\prime},D_{X}(L^{\prime}))}

where the bottom isomorphism comes from applying the functor
\mathrm{H}^{0}(X,.)
to the
isomorphism of Lemma
8.4
.

Proof.

As
\Perv_{mf}(X)
is a full subcategory of

\Perv_{h}(X)
, the morphism
R_{X}:\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,D_{X}(L))\longrightarrow\Hom_{\Perv_{h}(X)}(K^{\prime},D_{X}(L^{\prime}))
is an isomorphism. So we just need
to show that

R_{X}:\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,D_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}))\longrightarrow\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L^{\prime},K_{X})

is an isomorphism.
By Lemmas
8.5
and
8.6
, we have a commutative
diagram

\textstyle{\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)}(\mathrm{H}^{0}(a_{!}(K\otimes_{X}L)),1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{\Spec k}}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L^{\prime},K_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\Perv_{h}(\Spec k)}(\mathrm{H}^{0}(a_{!}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L^{\prime})),\underline{E}_{\Spec k})}

The right vertical map in this diagram is an isomorphism because
\Perv_{mf}(\Spec k)
is a full subcategory of
\Perv_{h}(\Spec k)
, so the left vertical
map is also an isomorphism.
∎

We use the formalism of filtered derived categories, which is recalled at the beginning
of Section
A.1
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category, and let
\DFb(\mathcal{A})
be its bounded filtered
derived category. Let us recall the spectral sequence of
[
BBD82
]

(3.1.3.4) : If
K
and
L
are two objects of
\DFb(A)
, then we have a spectral sequence

E_{1}^{pq}=\bigoplus_{j-i=p}\Ext^{p+q}_{\D(\mathcal{A})}(\Gr_{F}^{i}K,\Gr_{F}^{j}L)\Longrightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\D(\mathcal{A})}(\omega(K),\omega(L)).

Remember that
\omega:\DFb(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\Db(\mathcal{A})
is the functor that forgets the
filtration.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
K_{1}^{\bullet},K_{2}^{\bullet}
be bounded complexes
of objects of
\Perv_{h}(X)
, and let
K_{1},K_{2}
be their images by

\mathrm{real}:\Db\Perv_{h}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbh(X)
. Then, for every object
L
of
\Dbh(X)
,
we have a spectral sequence

E_{1}^{pq}=\bigoplus_{a-b=-p}\Ext^{q}_{\Dbh(X)}(K_{1}^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}^{b}),L)\Longrightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\Dbh(X)}(K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}),L).

Proof.

By definition of the category
\Dbh(X)
, it suffices to
prove the statement in
\Dbc(\mathcal{X})
, where
(A,\mathcal{X},u)
is an object of

\mathcal{U}X
such that all the
K^{i}_{r}
(resp.
L
) extend to shifts of objects of

\Perv(\mathcal{X})
(resp.
\Dbc(\mathcal{X})
), that we will denote by the same letters.

Remember the construction of the
realization functor
\Db\Perv(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\Dbc(\mathcal{X})
in Section
A.2
:
We consider the full subcategory

{\mathrm{DF}_{\text{b\^{e}te}}}(\mathcal{X})
of objects
A
of
\DFb(\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}})
such that
\Gr_{F}^{i}A[i]
is
in
\Perv(\mathcal{X})
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0
for
|i|
big enough.
We have an equivalence
G:{\mathrm{DF}_{\text{b\^{e}te}}}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow C^{b}(\Perv(\mathcal{X}))
(see

[
BBD82
]
3.1.7 or Theorem
A.2.3
), and
\mathrm{real}
is induced by

\omega\circ G^{-1}:C^{b}(\Perv(\mathcal{X}))\longrightarrow\Dbc(\mathcal{X})
.

Let
\Delta:X\longrightarrow X\times X
be the diagonal morphism. As

K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2})=\Delta^{*}(K_{1}\boxtimes D_{X}(K_{2}))
, we have a canonical
isomorphism

R\Hom_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X})}(K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}),L)=R\Hom_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})}(K_{1}\boxtimes D_{X}(K_{2}),\Delta_{*}L).

Let
M=G^{-1}(K_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes D_{X}(K_{2}^{\bullet}))\in\Ob{\mathrm{DF}_{\text{b\^{e}te}}}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})
.
We can also see
\Delta_{*}L
as an
object of
\DF((\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}})
(because, for any abelian
category
\mathcal{A}
, the category
\Db(\mathcal{A})
is canonically
equivalent to the full subcategory of
A\in\Ob\DFb(\mathcal{A}))
such that

\Gr_{F}^{i}A=0
for
i\not=0
). Using the spectral sequence recalled
before the statement of the lemma (and (iii) of Proposition

2.3.1
), we get a spectral sequence

E_{1}^{pq}=\bigoplus_{-i=p}\Ext^{p+q}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})}(\Gr_{F}^{i}(M),\Delta_{*}L)\Longrightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X})}(K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}),L).

For every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\Gr_{F}^{i}M=\bigoplus_{a+b=i}K_{1}^{a}[-a]\boxtimes D_{X}(K_{2}^{-b})[-b]=\bigoplus_{a-b=i}(K^{a}\boxtimes D_{X}(K^{b}))[-i].

So

E_{1}^{pq}=\bigoplus_{a-b=-p}\Ext^{p+q}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X})}(K_{1}^{a}\boxtimes D_{X}(K_{2}^{b}),\Delta_{*}[-p])=\bigoplus_{a-b=-p}\Ext^{p}_{\Dbc(\mathcal{X})}(K^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{b}),L).

The statement of the lemma now follows by taking the limit over
A^{\prime}
, with
A\subset A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
K_{1}^{\bullet},K_{2}^{\bullet}
be bounded complexes
of objects of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
, and let
K_{1},K_{2}
be their images by the canonical
functor

\Cb\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
. Then, for every object
L
of

\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
,
we have a spectral sequence

E_{1}^{pq}=\bigoplus_{a-b=-p}\Ext^{q}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K_{1}^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}^{b}),L)\Longrightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}),L).

Moreover, the functor
R_{X}
induces a morphism of spectral sequences
from this spectral sequence to the one of Lemma
8.8
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as for Lemma
8.8
,
except that we work in the filtered derived category

\DFb(\Perv_{mf}(X\times X))
. The last statement is obvious.
∎

Notation 8.10
.

Let
K\in\Ob\Dbh(X)
. We denote
by
\iota_{K}
the evaluation
morphism

K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K)=K\otimes_{X}\Homf_{X}(K,K_{X})\longrightarrow K_{X}.

This morphism satisfies the following naturality property: If
u:K\longrightarrow L
is a morphism in

\Dbh(X)
, then we get a commutative square

\textstyle{K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(u)}
\scriptstyle{u\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{K}}
\textstyle{L\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{L}}
\textstyle{K_{X}}

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
K_{1}^{\bullet},K_{2}^{\bullet}
be bounded complexes
of objects of
\Perv_{h}(X)
, and let
K_{1},K_{2}
be their images by the functor

\mathrm{real}:\Db\Perv_{h}(X)\longrightarrow\Dbh(X)
. Let

E_{1}^{pq}\Longrightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\Dbh(X)}(K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{2}),K_{X})

be the spectral sequence of Lemma
8.8
for
L=K_{X}
.

Then
E_{1}^{pq}=0
if
q<0
. Moreover, if
K_{1}^{\bullet}=K_{2}^{\bullet}=K^{\bullet}
and we write
K=K_{1}
,
then the element
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}
of
E_{1}^{00}
is in
\Ker(E_{1}^{00}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{10})=E_{2}^{00}
,
and the element
\iota_{K}

of
\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K),K_{X})\supset E_{\infty}^{00}
is the image
of
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}

by the map
E_{2}^{00}\longrightarrow E_{\infty}^{00}
.

Proof.

We have

E_{1}^{pq}=\bigoplus_{a-b=-p}\Ext^{q}_{\Dbh(X)}(K^{a}_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{b}_{2}),K_{X}).

As all the
K^{a}_{1}
and
D_{X}(K^{b}_{2})
are perverse, this is
0
for

q<0
by Lemma
8.5
.
This implies that
E_{2}^{00}=\Ker(E_{1}^{00}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{10})
and
that
E_{r}^{pq}=0
for any
r\geq 1
and any
q<0
, so

E_{\infty}^{pq}=0
for
q<0
. In particular, we get that

E_{\infty}^{00}
is a quotient of
E_{2}^{00}
and that

E_{\infty}^{00}\subset\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K_{1}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K_{1}),K_{X})
.
The last statement now follows from the construction of the spectral
sequence (and (iii) of Proposition
2.3.1
).
∎

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
K^{\bullet}
be a bounded complex of
objects of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
, and let
K\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
be its
image by the obvious functor
C^{b}\Perv_{mf}(X)\longrightarrow\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.
Then there exists a unique morphism
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}:K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K)\longrightarrow a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}
satisfying the following
conditions :

(a)

The image of
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}
by
R_{X}
is the morphism

\iota_{R_{X}(K)}
of
8.10
.

(b)

The analogue of Lemma
8.11
holds if we use
the spectral sequence of Lemma
8.9
.

Moreover, if
u^{\bullet}:K^{\bullet}\longrightarrow L^{\bullet}
is a morphism in
\Cb\Perv_{mf}(X)
and if

u:K\longrightarrow L
is its image in
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
, then
the following square commutes:

(*)

\textstyle{K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(u)}
\scriptstyle{u\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{K^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{L\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{L^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{K_{X}}

Proof.

Let
{K^{\prime}}^{\bullet}=R_{X}(K^{\bullet})
and
K^{\prime}=R_{X}(K)
.
If
K^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degree
0
, then, by
Lemma
8.7
, the morphism

R_{X}:\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K),D_{X}(1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}))\longrightarrow\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{\prime}),K_{X})

is an isomorphism. So condition (a) forces us to take
\iota_{K}=R_{X}^{-1}(\iota_{K^{\prime}})
, and condition (b) is trivial in this case.

We now construct
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}
in the general case. The spectral sequence of Lemma

8.9
for
L=a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
is

E_{1}^{pq}=\Ext^{q}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{b}),a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})\Longrightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K),a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}).

We have
E_{1}^{pq}=0
for
q<0
by Lemma
8.6
.
As in the proof of Lemma
8.11
, this implies that

E_{2}^{00}=\Ker(E_{1}^{00}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{10})
surjects to
E_{\infty}^{00}

and that
E_{\infty}^{00}\subset\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K),a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})
.
By condition (b), the element
\iota_{K}\in\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K),a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})
that we want to construct must be the image of

\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}\in E_{1}^{00}
. As
\iota_{K^{a}}
exists and is
uniquely determined by the first case, it suffices to show
that
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}\in\Ker(E_{1}^{00}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{10})
. Indeed,
condition (a) will then follow from the fact that
R_{X}
induces a morphism
between the spectral sequences of Lemmas
8.8

and
8.9
(and from Lemma
8.11
).
We denote by

E_{1}^{pq}(K^{\prime})
the spectral sequence of Lemma
8.8
for
{K^{\prime}}^{\bullet}
.
Then we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{E_{1}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{10}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{X}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{00}(K^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{10}(K^{\prime})}

By Lemma
8.7
, the vertical maps in this diagram are
isomorphisms. By Lemma
8.11
, the image
by
R_{X}
of
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}\in E_{1}^{00}
, which is

\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{{K^{\prime}}^{a}}
by construction of the
\iota_{K^{a}}
, is
in
\Ker(E_{1}^{00}(K^{\prime})\longrightarrow E_{1}^{10}(K^{\prime}))
. So

\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}
is in
\Ker(E_{1}^{00}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{10})
, and we are
done.

We prove the last statement. If
K^{\bullet}
and
L^{\bullet}
are both concentrated in degree
0
, then
K
and
L

are perverse, so the commutativity of (*) follows from the commutativity of the square we get applying
R_{X}
and
from Lemma
8.7
. We treat the general case. For
K_{1}^{\bullet},K_{2}^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb\Perv_{mf}(X)
, we denote
by
{}_{K_{1}^{\bullet},K_{2}^{\bullet}}E_{\bullet}^{\bullet,\bullet}
the spectral sequence of Lemma
8.9
for
K_{1}^{\bullet}
,
K_{2}^{\bullet}
and
K_{X}
.
The morphisms
u^{\bullet}
and
D_{X}(u^{\bullet})
induce morphisms of spectral sequence

{}_{L^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{\bullet}^{\bullet,\bullet}\longrightarrow{}_{K^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{\bullet}^{\bullet,\bullet}\longleftarrow{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{\bullet}^{\bullet,\bullet}

that are compatible with the morphisms
\Ext^{\bullet}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(L,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Ext^{\bullet}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{*}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\Ext^{\bullet}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,K)
.
So we get the commutative diagram (**), where
\mathrm{H}(\cdot)
means
\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\cdot,K_{X})
.
We have
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}\in\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K))
, and its image in
\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L))
by the arrow in the last row of (**) is
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(u))
. Similarly,
we have
\iota_{L^{\bullet}}\in\mathrm{H}(L\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L))
, and its image in
\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L))
by the arrow in the last row of (**) is
\iota_{L^{\bullet}}\circ(u\otimes_{X}\mathrm{id})
.
On the other hand, during the construction of
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}
, we proved that the element
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}
of
{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}
comes from a (unique)
element
e_{K^{\bullet}}
of
{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{2}^{00}
, and that
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}
is the image of
e_{K^{\bullet}}
in
H(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K))
; we have a similar statement for
\iota_{L^{\bullet}}
.
By the commutativity of (**), it suffices to prove that the images of
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}\in{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}
and
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{L^{a}}\in{}_{L^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}
in

{}_{K^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}
by the maps of the second row of (**) are equal. But the image of the first element is
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{K^{a}}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(u^{a}))
,
and the image of the second element is
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\iota_{L^{a}}\circ(u^{a}\otimes_{X}\mathrm{id})
. So the equality of these images follows from the case where
K^{\bullet}
and
L^{\bullet}
are
concentrated in degree
0
, which we already treated.

(**)

\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{H}(L^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L^{a}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{H}(K^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L^{a}))}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{H}(K^{a}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{a}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{L^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{K^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{1}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{L^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{2}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{K^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{2}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{2}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{L^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{\infty}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{K^{\bullet},L^{\bullet}}E_{\infty}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{K^{\bullet},K^{\bullet}}E_{\infty}^{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(L\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(L))}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

∎

The last statement of Lemma
8.12
implies in particular that, for

K\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
, the morphism
\iota_{K^{\bullet}}:K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K)\longrightarrow K_{X}
does not
depend on the complex
K^{\bullet}\in\Ob\Cb\Perv_{mf}(X)
representing
K
. We denote this
morphism by
\iota_{K}
.

Lemma 8.13
.

For
K,L\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
, we define
a morphism

u_{K,L}:R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(L,D_{X}(K))\longrightarrow R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})

as the composition of

K\otimes_{X}(.):R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(L,D_{X}(K))\longrightarrow R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K))

and of

\iota_{K*}:R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K))\longrightarrow R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}).

Then this morphism is natural in
K
and
L
, its image
by
R_{X}
is the adjunction morphism

R\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(R_{X}(L),D_{X}(R_{X}(K)))=R\Hom_{\Dbh(X)}(R_{X}(K)\otimes_{X}R_{X}(L),K_{X}),

and it is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The naturality in
L
is obvious and the second statement
follows from property (a) of Lemma
8.12
.

We prove the naturality in
K
. Let
u:K\longrightarrow K^{\prime}
be a morphism in
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.
We consider the diagram, where we write
\mathrm{H}
for
R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}\Perv(X)}

\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(L,D_{X}(K))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{K\otimes_{X}(.)}
\scriptstyle{(1)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}L,K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{K*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}L,K\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(u))_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(u\otimes_{X}\mathrm{id})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(3)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K\otimes_{X}L,K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{K^{\prime}*}}
\scriptstyle{(4)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(L,D_{X}(K^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{X}(u)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}(.)}
\scriptstyle{K\otimes_{X}(.)}
\scriptstyle{(2)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L,K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}D_{X}(K^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{K^{\prime}*}}
\scriptstyle{(u\otimes_{X}\mathrm{id})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}(K^{\prime}\otimes_{X}L,K_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(u\otimes_{X}\mathrm{id})^{*}}

Squares (1), (2) and (4) in this diagram clearly commute, and square (3) commutes
by the last statement of Lemma
8.12
. So the exterior rectangle commutes, which is what we needed to prove.

We turn to the proof of the third statement, i.e. that
u_{K,L}
is an isomorphism.
We first prove it in the
case where
X
is smooth and connected and
K=\mathcal{L}
,
L=\mathcal{M}
are
lisse sheaves on
X
.
Let
d=\dim(X)
. Then we have
a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}=1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}[2d](d)

(by Proposition
2.4.2
(i))
and

D_{X}(\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{L}^{*}[2d](d)
, where
\mathcal{L}^{*}=\Homf(\mathcal{L},\underline{E}_{X})
is the
dual locally constant sheaf (by the calculation at the end of
section
2.1
and Proposition

2.5.2
).
So
u_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}}
is a morphism

R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L}^{*})\longrightarrow R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M},1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}),

and the morphism
\iota_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{L}\otimes D_{X}(\mathcal{L})\longrightarrow a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}
of
Lemma
8.12
is
just the the canonical morphism
\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}^{*}\longrightarrow 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
, shifted by
2d

and twisted by
d
(we see this easily from conditions (a) and (b)
of Lemma
8.12
, as
\mathcal{L}
is perverse up
to a shift).
We will use the Yoneda description of the
\Ext^{k}
groups, as in
section 3.2 of chapter III of Verdier’s book
[
Ver96
]
.
The definition of
u_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}}
gives the following
formula for the image of a class
c
in

\Ext^{i}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L}^{*})=\Ext^{i}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{M}[d],\mathcal{L}^{*}[d])\ :

Choose an
exact sequence in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
representing
c
, say :

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}[d]\longrightarrow K_{i-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow K_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow 0.

Tensoring this sequence by
\mathcal{L}
, we still get an exact sequence in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
:

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}^{*}[d]\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}K_{i-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}K_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow 0.

Then
u_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}}(c)
is represented by the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}[d]\longrightarrow K^{\prime}_{i-1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}K_{i-2}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}K_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow 0,

where
K^{\prime}_{i-1}
is the amalgamated sum

1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}[d]\oplus_{\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}^{*}[d]}(\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}K_{i-1})

with the morphism
\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}^{*}[d]\longrightarrow 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}[d]

being the shift of the obvious one.
We want to show that
u_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}}
is bijective, so it suffices to construct its
inverse. Suppose that
c^{\prime}
is an element of

\Ext^{i}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M},1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X})=\Ext^{i}_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d],1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}[d]),

and choose an exact sequence in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
representing
c^{\prime}
, say :

0\longrightarrow 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}[d]\longrightarrow L_{i-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow 0.

Tensoring this sequence by
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, we still get an exact sequence in

\Perv_{mf}(X)
:

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}[d]\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}L_{i-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}L_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow 0.

We send
c^{\prime}
to the element of
\Ext^{i}_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(\mathcal{M}[d],\mathcal{L}^{*}[d])

represented by the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}[d]\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}L_{i-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}L_{1}\longrightarrow L^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow 0,

where
L^{\prime}_{0}
is the fiber product

(\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}L_{0})\times_{\mathcal{M}[d]}(\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d])

with the morphism
\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{M}[d]\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}[d]

coming from
\mathcal{L}^{*}\otimes_{X}\mathcal{L}\longrightarrow 1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{X}
by tensoring by
\mathcal{M}[d]
. This is
clearly the inverse of
u_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}}
.

Now we show that the morphism
u_{K,L}
is an
isomorphism for all
K,L\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.
Note the following two reductions : First,
using the fact that all the functors are triangulated and the five lemma,
we see that if we have an
exact triangle

K^{\prime}\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow K^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}

such that the result is true for
(K^{\prime},L)
and
(K^{\prime\prime},L)
, then the result
if true for
(K,L)
. There is a similar statement for the second variable
L
.
So it suffices to prove the result for
K
and
L
concentrated in
perverse degree
0
,
and we may also assume that
K
and
L
are simple perverse sheaves.
Second, suppose that we have a closed immersion
i:Y\longrightarrow X
, and let

j:U:=X-Y\longrightarrow X
be the complementary open immersion. Then we have
a commutative diagram whose columns are distinguished triangles (all the

R\Hom
s are taken in the appropriate category
\Db\Perv_{mf}(Z)
, with
Z\in\{X,U,Y\}
) :

\textstyle{R\Hom(i^{*}L,i^{!}D_{X}K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}u_{K,L}}
\textstyle{R\Hom(i^{*}(K\otimes_{X}L),i^{!}a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\Hom(L,D_{X}K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{K,L}}
\textstyle{R\Hom(K\otimes_{X}L,a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\Hom(j^{*}L,j^{*}D_{X}K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}u_{K,L}}
\textstyle{R\Hom(j^{*}(K\otimes_{X}L),j^{*}a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}

Moreover, using the compatibility of
\otimes_{X}
with inverse images
and point (ii) of Proposition
7.3.2
, we get isomorphisms :

R\Hom(i^{*}(K\otimes_{X}L),i^{!}a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\simeq R\Hom((i^{*}K)\otimes_{Y}(i^{*}L),a_{Y}^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})

and

R\Hom(j^{*}(K\otimes_{X}L),j^{*}a^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k})\simeq R\Hom((j^{*}K)\otimes_{U}(j^{*}L),a_{U}^{!}1\!\!\mkern-1.0mu1_{\Spec k}),

where
a_{Y}=a\circ i
and
a_{U}=a\circ j
.
It is easy to see that these isomorphisms identify
i^{*}u_{K,L}

(resp.
j^{*}u_{K,L}
) with
u_{i^{*}K,i^{*}L}
(resp.
u_{j^{*}K,j^{*}L}
).
So the result for
X
follows from the result for
Y
and
U
.

Using the two reductions above and Noetherian induction on
X
, we can reduce
to the case where
X
is smooth and
K
and
L
are both shifts of locally
constant sheaves on
X
. But this case has already been treated in the first
part of the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

We have to construct an isomorphism

R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K\otimes_{X}L,M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R\Hom_{\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)}(L,D_{X}(K\otimes_{X}D_{X}M))

functorial in
K,L,M\in\Ob\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
and compatible (via
R_{X}
)
with the adjunction morphism in
\Dbh(X)
.
But such an isomorphism is
given by

u_{L,K\otimes_{X}D_{X}M}\circ u_{K\otimes_{X}L,D_{X}M}^{-1},

where
u_{.,.}
is constructed in Lemma
8.13
.
∎

9
Weight filtration on complexes

The goal of this section is to generalize the results of section 3
of
[
Mor08
]
, and in particular the formula for the intermediate extension
of a pure perverse sheaf, to the categories
\Perv_{mf}(X)
and their derived
categories.
This was the original
motivation for considering the categories
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
X
be a
k
-scheme. For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
,
we denote by
{}^{w}\D^{\leq a}(X)
(resp.
{}^{w}\D^{\geq a}(X)
) the full subcategory
of
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
whose objects are the complexes
K
such that,
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathrm{H}^{i}K\in\Perv_{mf}(X)
is of weight
\leq a
(resp.

\geq a
).

Note that
{}^{w}\D^{\leq a}(X)
and
{}^{w}\D^{\geq a}(X)
are triangulated subcategories
of
\Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
K,L\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(X)
. Suppose that there exists

a\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
K
is of weight
\leq a
and
L
is of weight
\geq a+1
.
Then we have, for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\Ext^{i}_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,L)=0.

For categories like that of mixed Hodge modules,
this result follows from Lemma 6.9 of
[
Sai91
]
, but M. Saito assumes (and uses)
the fact that pure objects are semisimple, which is false in our case.

Proof.

We obviously have
\Ext^{i}_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,L)=0
if
i<0
,
and
\Hom_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,L)=0
because the weights of
K
and
L
are disjoint.
We denote by
W
the weight filtration on objects of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.
For every
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get an endofunctor
W_{b}
of
\Perv_{mf}(X)
, which
is exact because weight filtrations are
strictly compatible with morphisms in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
(by Lemma 3.8 of

[
Hub97
]
).

As in the proof of Proposition
8.3
,
we will use the Yoneda description of the
\Ext^{k}
groups (see
section 3.2 of chapter III of Verdier’s book
[
Ver96
]
).
Let
i\geq 1
and
let
\alpha\in\Ext^{i}_{\Perv_{mf}(X)}(K,L)
. Choose an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{i-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{i-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ldots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K\longrightarrow 0

in
\Perv_{mf}(X)
that represents
\alpha
. Applying
W_{a}
to this exact
sequence and using the fact that
W_{a}K=K
and
W_{a}L=0
,
we get a morphism of exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{i}}
\textstyle{M_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{i-1}}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{0}}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{L}+0}
\scriptstyle{u+\can}
\textstyle{L\oplus W_{a}M_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0+u_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{\can}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{1}}
\textstyle{W_{a}M_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\can}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
\can:W_{a}\longrightarrow\mathrm{id}
is the canonical inclusion.
So the class
\alpha
is also represented by the second row of this
diagram, hence it is trivial.
∎

Corollary 9.3
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, the pair

({}^{w}\D^{\leq a},{}^{w}\D^{\geq a+1})
is a t-structure on
\\
Db\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

We denote by
w_{\leq a}
and
w_{\geq a+1}
the truncation functors for this
t-structure. They extend the exact functors
K\longmapsto W_{a}K
and
K\longmapsto K/W_{a}K
on
\Perv_{mf}(X)
.

Proof.

Once we have the vanishing result of Proposition
9.2
, the proofs
of Lemmas 3.2.1 and 3.2.2 of
[
Mor08
]
apply without modification.
∎

Corollary 9.4
.

The results of sections 3 and 5.1 of
[
Mor08
]
are still
true in our situation. In particular, if
j:U\longrightarrow X
is an open immersion
of
k
-schemes and
K\in\Ob\Perv_{mf}(U)
is pure of weight
a
, then the
canonical morphisms

w_{\geq a}j_{!}K\longrightarrow j_{!*}K\longrightarrow w_{\leq a}j_{*}K

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

The proofs of
[
Mor08
]
apply without modification.
∎

Appendix A
Filtered derived categories and f-categories

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and let
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
be a t-structure on
\mathcal{D}
with heart
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}
.
Under appropriate hypotheses, the inclusion
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
extends to a triangulated functor
\mathrm{real}:\Db(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
called the
realization functor
; the first goal of this appendix
is to explain how to construct this extension (see Theorem
A.2.3
).

Now suppose that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is another triangulated category, that
({\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}^{\leq 0},{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}^{\geq 0})
is a t-structure with heart

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and that
T:\mathcal{D}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a triangulated functor. Under appropriate hypotheses
(see Theorem
A.2.3
), we will have realization
functors
\mathrm{real}:\Db(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
and
\mathrm{real}:\Db(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. The second goal of this appendix is, supposing that there are “enough”
T
-acyclic objects in
\mathcal{C}
,
to extend
\mathrm{H}^{0}T:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
to a triangulated functor
DT:\Db(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\Db(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
such that
\mathrm{real}\circ DT\simeq T\circ\mathrm{real}
; the functor
DT
should be the restriction
to
\Db(\mathcal{C})
of the derived functor of
\mathrm{H}^{0}T
if
T
is left or right t-exact. See Proposition
A.3.2
and Remark
A.3.3
for precise statements.

The technical tool that we need for the two goals above is the formalism of f-categories over triangulated categories (introduced by Beilinson in Appendix A of
[
Beĭ87b
]
),
which abstracts the properties of filtered derived categories. So we start with a review of this formalism.

A.1
Review of f-categories and f-functors

Filtered derived categories

We recall the definition of filtered derived categories, as they are the motivation for the definition of f-categories, and also
the main example of this formalism.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category.
We denote by
\Fil(\mathcal{A})
category of filtered objects of
\mathcal{A}
, where
filtrations are assumed to be decreasing; it has a full subcategory
\Filf(\mathcal{A})
, whose objects are
filtered objects of
\mathcal{A}
whose filtration is finite, which means in particular that it is separated and exhaustive.
Both
\Fil(\mathcal{A})
and
\Filf(\mathcal{A})
are quasi-abelian categories.
We denote by
\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
(resp.
\Dpl(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
,
\D^{-}(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
,
\Db(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
) the unbounded (resp. bounded above, bounded below, bounded)
derived category of
\Filf(\mathcal{A})
(see for example Definition 2.3 of Schapira and
Schneiders’s
[
SS16
]
, or
[
Sta24
,
Definition 05S2
]
and

[
Sta24
,
Definition 05S4
]
; note that the definitions of the variously bounded derived
categories in
[
SS16
]
and in the Stacks Project are a priori different, but they define the same subcategories by
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 05S5
]
).
Following the convention of Section 3.1 of
[
BBD82
]
and Chapter V of Illusie’s
[
Ill71
]
, we define the
the
filtered derived category

\DF(\mathcal{A})
(resp. the
bounded above filtered derived category

\DFpl(\mathcal{A})
, etc) of
\mathcal{A}
to be the full subcategory of
\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
(resp.

\Dpl(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
etc) of complexes whose filtration is finite, and not just finite in each degree.

As the filtration on a finite complex of objects of
\Filf(\mathcal{A})
is always finite, we have
\DFb(\mathcal{A})=\Db(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
, so the definitions of

[
Sta24
,
Section 05RX
]
,
[
BBD82
]
and
[
Ill71
]
coincide for the bounded filtered
derived category.

We denote objects of
\Filf(\mathcal{A})
and
\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
by
(K,F^{\bullet})
, or often just
K
.

We have additive functors:

•

s:\Filf(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\Filf(\mathcal{A})
,
(K,F^{\bullet})\mapsto(K,F^{\bullet-1})
;

•

\Fil^{r}:\Filf(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\Filf(\mathcal{A})
sending
(K,F^{\bullet})
to
F^{r}
with the filtration induced by
F^{\bullet}
;

•

(.)/\Fil^{r}:\Filf(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\Filf(\mathcal{A})
sending
(K,F^{\bullet})
to
K/F^{r}K
with the filtration induced by
F^{\bullet}
;

•

\Gr^{r}:\Filf(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}
,
(K,\Fil^{\bullet})\longmapsto\Fil^{r}K/\Fil^{r-1}K
;

•

\Gr=\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\Gr^{r}:\Filf(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}
;

•

\omega:\Fil(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}
,
(K,F^{\bullet})\longmapsto K
;

•

i:\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\Filf(\mathcal{A})
sending
K\in\Ob(\mathcal{A})
to
K
with the trivial filtration
F^{\bullet}
(
F^{r}=K
for
r\leq 0
and
F^{r}=0
for
r\geq 1
).

These induce functors on the categories of complexes, and, as in

[
Sta24
,
Lemma 05S3
]
, we get triangulated functors

s,\sigma_{\leq r},\sigma_{\geq r+1}:\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))\longrightarrow\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
,

\Gr^{r},\Gr:\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))\longrightarrow\D(\mathcal{A})

\omega:\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))\longrightarrow\D(\mathcal{A})
and
i:\D(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
. For
?\in\{+,-,b\}
, these functors send to
\D^{?}(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
(resp.
\D^{?}(\mathcal{A})
) to
\D^{?}(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
(resp.
\D^{?}(\mathcal{A})
), and
they also respect the various filtered derived categories (when it makes sense, for example
s
sends
\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
to
\DF?(\mathcal{A})
, and
i
sends
\D^{?}(\mathcal{A})
to
\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
).
For every
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
a natural transformation
\Gr^{r}\longrightarrow\Gr^{r+1}[1]
(coming from the triangle

\Gr^{r+1}K\longrightarrow F^{r}K/F^{r+2}K\longrightarrow\Gr^{r}K
, if
(K,F^{\bullet})
is an object
of
\D(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
). We also have a natural transformation
\alpha:\mathrm{id}_{\DF(\mathcal{A})}\longrightarrow s
coming from the inclusions
F^{r}K\subset F^{r-1}K
, for
(K,F^{\bullet})\in\Ob\Filf(\mathcal{A})
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

\DF(\leq n)=\{K\in\Ob\DF(\mathcal{A})\mid\forall r\geq n+1,\ \Gr^{r}K=0\}

and

\DF(\geq n)=\{K\in\Ob\DF(\mathcal{A})\mid\forall r\leq n-1,\ \Gr^{r}K=0\};

these are full subtriangulated categories of
\DF(\mathcal{A})
, stable by isomorphisms.
Thanks to our convention on filtered derived categories, we have
\DF(\mathcal{A})=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\DF(\leq n)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\DF(\geq n)
.
Similarly, if
?\in\{+,-,b\}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set

\DF^{?}(\leq n)=\DF(\leq n)\cap\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
and
\DF^{?}(\geq n)=\DF(\geq n)\cap\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
.
For
?\in\{\varnothing,+,-,b\}
, the functor
i
induces an equivalence of categories
\D^{?}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\DF^{?}(\leq 0)\cap\DF^{?}(\geq 0)
.

f-categories

In Appendix A of
[
Beĭ87b
]
, Beilinson introduced f-categories over triangulated categories, that have all the abstract properties of filtered
derived categories, and generalized the properties of filtered derived categories to this more general setting. We review his definition and results.
Note that Section 6 of Schnürer’s paper
[
Sch11
]
gives more detailed proofs of many of the results of Appendix A of
[
Beĭ87b
]
.

Definition A.1.1

(Definition A.1 of
[
Beĭ87b
]
)
.

(1)

A
filtered triangulated category
, or
f-category
, is the data of:

–

a triangulated category
\DF
;

–

two full triangulated subcategories
\DF(\leq 0)
,
\DF(\geq 0)
of
\DF
that are stable by isomorphisms;

–

a triangulated self-equivalence
s:\DF\longrightarrow\DF
(called
shift of filtration
);

–

a morphism of functors
\alpha:\mathrm{id}_{\DF}\longrightarrow s
;

satisfying the following conditions, where, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set

\DF(\leq n)=s^{n}\DF(\leq 0)\mbox{ and }\DF(\geq n)=s^{n}\DF(\geq 0):

(i)

We have
\DF(\geq 1)\subset\DF(\geq 0)
,
\DF(\leq 1)\supset\DF(\leq 0)
and

\DF=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\DF(\leq n)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\DF(\geq n).

(ii)

For any
X\in\Ob\DF
, we have
\alpha_{X}=s(\alpha_{s^{-1}(X)})
.

(iii)

For any
X\in\Ob\DF(\geq 1)
and
Y\in\Ob\DF(\leq 0)
, we have
\Hom(X,Y)=0
, and the maps
\Hom(s(Y),X)\longrightarrow\Hom(Y,X)\longrightarrow\Hom(Y,s^{-1}(X))
induced
by
\alpha_{Y}
and
\alpha_{s^{-1}(X)}
are bijective.

(iv)

For every
X\in\Ob\DF
, there exists a distinguished triangle
A\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
with
A
in
\DF(\geq 1)
and
B
in
\DF(\leq 0)
.

(2)

If
\DF
and
\DF^{\prime}
are f-categories, an
f-functor
from
\DF
to
\DF^{\prime}
is the data of a triangulated functor
T:\DF\longrightarrow\DF^{\prime}
and a natural isomorphism

s^{\prime}\circ T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}T\circ s

such that
T(\DF(\leq 0))\subset\DF^{\prime}(\leq 0)
,

T(\DF(\geq 0))\subset\DF(\geq 0)
and that, for every
X\in\Ob\DF
, the following triangle commutes:

\textstyle{T(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}_{T(X)}}
\scriptstyle{T(\alpha(X))}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}(T(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{T(s(X))}

(3)

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category. An
f-category over
\mathcal{D}
is an f-category
\DF
together with an equivalence
i:\mathcal{D}\longrightarrow\DF(\leq 0)\cap\DF(\geq 0)
.
If
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is another triangulated category,
\DF^{\prime}
is an
f
-category over
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and
T:\mathcal{D}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a triangulated functor, an
f-lifting
of
T
is an f-functor

FT:\DF\longrightarrow\DF^{\prime}
and a natural isomorphism
i^{\prime}\circ T\simeq TF\circ i
.

Example A.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. For
?\in\{\varnothing,+,-,b\}
, the category
\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
with the subcategories
\DF^{?}(\leq 0)
and
\DF^{?}(\geq 0)
, the functors

s
and
i
and the natural transformation
\alpha
, is an f-category over the triangulated category
\D^{?}(\mathcal{A})
. Note that this is not be true for

\D^{?}(\Filf(\mathcal{A}))
instead of
\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
(except of course if
?=b
), because the third statement of condition (i) of Definition
A.1.1
does not hold.

Proposition A.1.3

(Proposition A.3 of
[
Beĭ87b
]
)
.

Let
\DF
be an f-category.

(i)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the inclusion
\DF(\leq n)\subset\DF
admits a left adjoint
\sigma_{\leq n}
, and the inclusion
\DF(\geq n)\subset\DF
admits a right adjoint
\sigma_{\geq n}
.
The functors
\sigma_{\leq n}
,
\sigma_{\geq n}
are triangulated and preserve the subcategories
\DF(\leq m)
,
\DF(\geq m)
for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii)

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a unique isomorphism of functors
\sigma_{\leq a}\sigma_{\geq b}\simeq\sigma_{\geq b}\sigma_{\leq a}
that makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\sigma_{\geq b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\DF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sigma_{\leq a}}
\textstyle{\sigma_{\leq a}\sigma_{\geq b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\sigma_{\geq b}\sigma_{\leq a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(iii)

Let
X\in\Ob\DF
. Then there exists a unique morphism
\delta:\sigma_{\leq 0}X\longrightarrow\sigma_{\geq 1}[1]
making the triangle
\sigma_{\leq 1}X\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow\sigma_{\leq 0}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sigma_{\geq 1}X[1]

distinguished.
Any distinguished triangle
A\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}
with
A\in\Ob\DF(\geq 1)
and
B\in\Ob\DF(\leq 0)
admits a unique isomorphism to the triangle of the previous sentence.

(iv)

We have canonical isomorphisms
\sigma_{\leq n}\circ s=s\circ\sigma_{\leq n-1}
and
\sigma_{\geq n}\circ s=s\circ\sigma_{\geq n-1}
.

Point (iv) is not stated in Proposition A.3 of
[
Beĭ87b
]
but follows immediately from the fact that
s(\DF(\leq n-1))=\DF(\leq n)
(resp.
s(D(\geq n-1))=D(\geq n)
) and the uniqueness
of adjoints.

Definition A.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and
\DF
be an f-category over
\mathcal{D}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define a functor
\Gr^{n}:\DF\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
by

\Gr^{n}=i^{-1}\circ s^{-n}\circ\sigma_{\leq n}\sigma_{\geq n}
.

Proposition A.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and
\DF
be an f-category over
\mathcal{D}
.

(i)

For every
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have a natural isomorphism
\Gr^{r}\circ s=\Gr^{r-1}
.

(ii)

Let
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\Gr^{r}\circ i=0
if
r\neq 0
and
\Gr^{r}\circ i\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
if
r=0
.

(iii)

Let
r,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We have

\Gr^{r}\circ\sigma_{\leq n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gr^{r}&\mbox{if }r\leq n\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\Gr^{r}\circ\sigma_{\geq n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gr^{r}&\mbox{if }r\geq n\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

Proof.

Point (i) follows from Proposition
A.1.3
(iv), point (ii) from the fact that the image of
i
is contained in
\DF(\leq 0)\cap\DF(\geq 0)
, and point (iii)
from the definition of
\Gr^{r}
.
∎

Proposition A.1.6

(Proposition A.3 of
[
Beĭ87b
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and
\DF
be an f-category over
\mathcal{D}
.
Then there exists a triangulated functor
\omega:\DF\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that:
7
7

7

Note that there is a typo in Proposition A.3 of
[

]
: the left and right

[
Sch11
]
.

(a)

\omega_{\mid\DF(\leq 0)}:\DF(\leq 0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

;

;

is an isomorphism;

(d)

if

and

, then

is bijective.

Moreover,
\omega
is determined up to unique isomorphism by properties (a) and (c) (resp. (b) and (c)).

Remark A.1.7
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category,
?\in\{\varnothing,+,-,n\}
,
\DF=\DF^{?}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{D}=\D^{?}(\mathcal{A})
, then the functors
\sigma_{\leq n}
,
\sigma_{\geq n}
,
\Gr^{n}
and

\omega
are isomorphic to the ones defined in the first part of this section.

The following proposition follows easily from the definitions.

Proposition A.1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

) be an f-category over
